1 introduction générale [SOURCE: Page_64]

En l’absence de perturbations et de bruit de mesure, l’observateur adaptatif reconstruit fidèlement l’azote et les paramètres. Cette performance est illustrée à la Figure 1.12. [SOURCE: Page_64]

(a) Estimation de 𝑆                             (b) Estimation de 𝐵 [SOURCE: Page_64]

(c) Estimation de 𝑁                      (d) Estimation des 𝑘𝑖 , 𝑖 = 1, 3, 5 [SOURCE: Page_64]

Figure 1.12: Reconstruction de l’azote 𝑁 et estimation des paramètres 𝑘 1 , 𝑘 3 , 𝑘 5 à l’aide de l’observateur adaptatif (sans perturbation ni bruit). [SOURCE: Page_64]

Enfin, une comparaison en présence de bruit de mesure a été effectuée entre l’observateur adaptatif proposé et un observateur adaptatif à grand gain classique. Les résultats obtenus démontrent la robustesse accrue de notre approche face au bruit, confirmant ainsi ses performances supérieures par rapport aux méthodes classiques. Au début de cette section, nous avons précisé que l’objectif est de reconstruire l’azote disponible ainsi que les paramètres incertains (voir Chapitre 3) dans le sous-domaine E (cf. Figure 1.7), délimité par les contraintes de stress hydrique et azoté. Cette restriction constitue une simplification du problème de reconstruction, dans la mesure où, en dehors de ce domaine, le système ne présente pas de propriété d’observabilité uniforme. [SOURCE: Page_64]

64 [SOURCE: Page_64]

1 introduction générale [SOURCE: Page_65]

La section suivante explore une approche de reconstruction adaptée aux systèmes non linéaires affectés par des perturbations, possédant la propriété dite de observabilité pratique. [SOURCE: Page_65]

1.8 observateur pratique pour une classe de systèmes non linéaires [SOURCE: Page_65]

Nous considérons dans cette partie, la forme suivante du système (1.73) : [SOURCE: Page_65]

𝑥¤ (𝑡) = 𝑓 (𝑥 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑣 (𝑡)), 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+, (Σ)                         𝑦 (𝑡) = ℎ(𝑥 (𝑡)), 𝑥 (0) = 𝑥 0 [SOURCE: Page_65]

où 𝑥 ∈ X ⊂ ℝ𝑛 représente l’état et 𝑥 0 ∈ X est la condition initiale, 𝑦 ∈ Y ⊂ ℝ𝑝 est la sortie mesurée de (Σ), 𝑢 ∈ U ⊂ ℝ𝑚 est l’entrée i.e le contrôle et 𝑣 (𝑡) ∈ V ⊂ ℝ𝑞 est une perturbation externe, 𝑓 : X × U × V → ℝ𝑛 est une fonction continue, Lipschitzienne par rapport à 𝑥 et uniformément en (𝑢, 𝑣), ℎ : X → Y est une fonction continûment différentiable. Nous rappelons quelques éléments classiques en absence de perturbations (𝑣 ≡ 0). Pour 𝑥 0 ∈ X, 𝑢 ∈ U on désigne par 𝑇 (𝑥 0, 𝑢, 0) B 𝑇 (𝑥 0, 𝑢) le temps maximal d’existence de la trajectoire correspondante 𝑥 (𝑡, 𝑥 0, 𝑢, 0) B 𝑥𝑥 0,𝑢 de (Σ) sur X. Si 𝑇 (𝑥 0, 𝑢) = +∞ pour tout 𝑥 0 ∈ X, 𝑢 ∈ U, le système (Σ) est forward complete. [SOURCE: Page_65]

Remarque 1.15. Le système décrit en (Σ) est dit mono-entrée et mono-sortie (SISO en anglais) lorsque 𝑚 = 𝑝 = 1, et multi-entrées et multi-sorties (MIMO en anglais) lorsque 𝑚 > 1 et 𝑝 > 1. [SOURCE: Page_65]

Hypothèse 1.16. Étant donné 𝑇 > 0, 𝑥 0 ∈ X et 𝑢 ∈ U, la solution correspondante 𝑥𝑥 0,𝑢 de (Σ) est uniquement définie sur l’intervalle maximal [0,𝑇 (𝑥 0, 𝑢)] ⊂ [0,𝑇 ]. [SOURCE: Page_65]

En théorie du contrôle et en ingénierie des systèmes, le problème d’observation d’état pour un système dynamique consiste généralement à répondre aux deux questions suivantes : avec les informations disponibles sur les entrées et les sorties, est-il possible de reconstruire l’état complet du système sur un intervalle de temps donné, et comment peut-on concevoir un système dynamique permettant cette reconstruction complète ? Il s’agit d’un problème d’observabilité pour un système non standard. La réponse à la première interrogation constitue l’étude de l’observabilité, que nous rappelons dans la sous section suivante. [SOURCE: Page_65]

1.8.1 notion d’observabilité [SOURCE: Page_66]

Définition 1.17 (Application entrée-sortie). Pour 𝑥 0 ∈ X, l’application Σ𝑥 0 : U −→ Y 𝑢 ↦−→ Σ𝑥 0 (𝑢) B 𝑦𝑥 0,𝑢 est appelée application entrée-sortie de (Σ) en 𝑥 0 . On note Σ𝑥 0 (𝑢 (𝑡)) = 𝑦𝑥 0,𝑢 (𝑡). [SOURCE: Page_66]

Définition 1.18 (Indistinguabilité,[Hermann & Krener 1977]). Deux états initiaux 𝑥 0𝑎 ∈ X et 𝑥 0𝑏 ∈ X sont dits indistinguables pour le système (Σ) si, pour tout 𝑢 ∈ U, les sorties cor- respondantes 𝑦𝑥 0𝑎 ,𝑢 et 𝑦𝑥 𝑏 ,𝑢 sont identiques sur l’intervalle [0, min{𝑇 (𝑥 0𝑎 , 𝑢), 𝑇 (𝑥 0𝑏 , 𝑢)}[. 0 Autrement dit, pour chaque contrôle 𝑢 ∈ U, on a : Σ𝑥 0𝑎 (𝑢 (𝑡)) = Σ𝑥 𝑏 (𝑢 (𝑡)), 𝑡 ∈ [0, min{𝑇 (𝑥 0𝑎 , 𝑢), 𝑇 (𝑥 0𝑏 , 𝑢)}[. 0 [SOURCE: Page_66]

La définition stipule que deux points de l’espace d’état X (Σ) sont dits indistinguables si (Σ) produit exactement la même trajectoire de sortie lorsqu’il part de ces points comme conditions initiales. Dans le cas contraire, on dit qu’ils sont distinguables, c’est- à-dire s’il existe un contrôle 𝑢 ∈ U telles que les sorties 𝑦𝑥 0𝑎 ,𝑢 et 𝑦𝑥 𝑏 ,𝑢 correspondantes 0 aux trajectoires 𝑥𝑥 0𝑎 ,𝑢 et 𝑥𝑥 𝑏 ,𝑢 diffèrent sur l’intervalle [0, min{𝑇 (𝑥 0𝑎 , 𝑢), 𝑇 (𝑥 0𝑏 , 𝑢)}[ (voir 0 Figure 1.13). ou, de manière équivalente, si (1.75) ∀𝑥 0𝑎 , 𝑥 0𝑏 ∈ X, Σ𝑥 0𝑎 (𝑢 (𝑡)) = Σ𝑥 𝑏 (𝑢 (𝑡)) sur 0, min{𝑇 (𝑥 0𝑎 , 𝑢), 𝑇 (𝑥 0𝑏 , 𝑢)} ⇒ 𝑥 0𝑎 = 𝑥 0𝑏 .                                    0 [SOURCE: Page_67]

Le système est dit observable en temps 𝛼 (pour un 𝑢 donné) si la condition initiale 𝑥 0 peut être reconstruite de manière unique à partir des mesures de la sortie 𝑦 sur un intervalle de temps fini [0, 𝛼]. Cela implique l’existence d’une application Ω telle que l’état 𝑥 (𝑡) puisse être reconstitué comme suit : [SOURCE: Page_67]

𝑥 (𝑡) = Ω 𝑦𝑥 0,𝑢,0 ( [𝑡 − 𝛼, 𝑡]), 𝑢 ([𝑡 − 𝛼, 𝑡]) , 𝑡 ≥ 𝛼 .  (1.76) [SOURCE: Page_67]

Pour un système linéaire temps-invariant [SOURCE: Page_67]

𝑥¤ (𝑡) = 𝐴𝑥 (𝑡) + 𝐵𝑢 (𝑡), LTI                                       𝑦 (𝑡) = 𝐶𝑥 (𝑡), 𝑥 (0) = 𝑥 0 [SOURCE: Page_67]

où 𝑥 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 , 𝑢 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑚 , 𝑦 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑝 , 𝐴 ∈ 𝕄𝑛 (ℝ), 𝐵 ∈ 𝕄𝑛,𝑚 (ℝ), 𝐶 ∈ 𝕄𝑝,𝑛 (ℝ), l’observabilité ne dépend pas de la matrice 𝐵 et se caractérise par le critère de rang de la matrice d’observabilité de Kalman (Appendix B). Il est important de noter qu’une condition nécessaire mais non suffisante à l’existence d’un observateur de LTI est que toute paire de trajectoire (𝑥𝑥 0𝑎 ,𝑢 , 𝑥𝑥 𝑏 ,𝑢 ) indistinguable doit, au minimum, converger l’une 0 vers l’autre asymptotiquement. Dans ce cas, on dit que le système LTI est détectable ou vérifie la propriété de détectabilité. La notion de détectabilité se généralise comme suit : [SOURCE: Page_67]

Définition 1.20 (Détectabilité). Le système (Σ) est détectable si pour tous états initiaux 𝑥 0𝑎 , 𝑥 0𝑏 ∈ X et pour tout contrôle 𝑢 ∈ U les trajectoires 𝑥𝑥 0𝑎 ,𝑢 , 𝑥𝑥 𝑏 ,𝑢 sont définies sur ℝ+ 0 de sorte que [SOURCE: Page_67]

(1.77)                            Σ𝑥 0𝑎 (𝑢 (𝑡)) = Σ𝑥 𝑏 (𝑢 (𝑡))      ∀𝑡 ≥ 0 0 [SOURCE: Page_67]

vérifie [SOURCE: Page_67]

(1.78)                                    lim ∥𝑥𝑥 0𝑎 ,𝑢 − 𝑥𝑥 𝑏 ,𝑢 ∥ = 0. 𝑡→∞                0 [SOURCE: Page_67]

La détectabilité affaiblie ainsi le concept d’observabilité et stipule qu’en cas d’indistingua bilité, les trajectoires convergent nécessairement l’une vers l’autre de façon asymp- totique. Par ailleurs, les notions d’observabilité et de détectabilité pour les systèmes linéaires peuvent être caractérisées en ne considérant que les trajectoires dont la sortie reste constamment nulle, et ce, indépendamment du contrôle 𝑢. Cependant, Gauthier, [SOURCE: Page_67]

67 [SOURCE: Page_67]

1 introduction générale [SOURCE: Page_68]

Hammouri & Othman (1992) expliquent que ce n’est plus le cas pour des systèmes non linéaires où l’observabilité est étroitement liée à l’entrée car certaines entrées peuvent détruire l’observabilité du système. L’observabilité devient difficile lorsque les contrôles s’approchent de ces entrées dites singulières, c’est-à-dire des entrées qui ne permettent pas de distinguer deux états initiaux différents. Un contrôle 𝑢 ∈ U, qui distingue toute paire d’états initiaux distincts 𝑥 0𝑎 et 𝑥 0𝑏 en temps arbitrairement court, est appelé entrée universelle. Les mauvaises entrées rendent alors nécessaire la considération de la classe des systèmes observables pour toute entrée, c’est-à-dire les systèmes pour lesquels aucune mauvaise entrée n’existe, et qui sont dits uniformément observables pour toute entrée. Autrement, le système (Σ) est uniformément observable si toutes les entrées sont universelles. Différentes conditions, nécessaires ou suffisantes, ont été proposées dans la littérature pour caractériser l’observabilité d’un système non linéaire GKbook; [Bai et al. 2019]. Par ailleurs, la notion d’observabilité pour les systèmes non linéaires dépend non seulement du système lui-même, mais aussi de la nature des entrées appliquées [Gauthier & Bornard 1981]. Les systèmes non linéaires requièrent des approches plus complexes du fait que leur dynamique dépend intrinsèquement des entrées et des perturbations [Bernard 2017]. Face à ces difficultés, une stratégie couramment adoptée dans la littérature consiste à supposer l’observabilité uniforme par rapport aux entrées, ce qui simplifie la conception d’un observateur en dissociant l’estimation de l’état du choix des entrées. Ces conditions, souvent formulées sous des hypothèses spécifiques, visent à garantir la reconstruction unique de l’état du système malgré la présence de non-linéarités [Bai et al. 2019]. Sous ces hypothèses, la tâche de l’observateur est simplement de reconstruire le vecteur d’état, l’observabilité uniforme garantissant que le choix des entrées ne compromette pas l’observabilité. Cependant, de nombreux systèmes pratiques ne satisfont pas à cette condition stricte. Par exemple, lorsque le stress hydrique est sévère, c’est-à-dire lorsque 0 < 𝑆 < 𝑆𝑤 (voir Figure 1.7), la reconstruction de l’azote initial est impossible en raison de l’inobservabilité uniforme du système dans cette zone. Lorsque le système est soumis à des perturbations inconnus, le problème est encore complexe et la relation 1.76 peut dépendre des perturbations. Les systèmes peuvent inclure des incertitudes bornées, des non-linéarités non inversibles, ou des gains singuliers variant dans le temps, autant d’éléments qui empêchent l’application directe des critères classiques d’observabilité. 𝑞 En présence d’une perturbation (𝑣 . 0) inconnue bornée (𝑣 ∈ L∞ ), nous avons proposé, dans le Chapitre 4, une approche permettant une estimation de l’état avec une erreur également bornée. L’idée consiste à garantir que l’erreur d’estimation reste contenue dans une boule de rayon 𝜀 centrée autour de la trajectoire réelle de l’état. Pour ce faire, nous proposons de relâcher la propriété (1.76). Plus précisément, les signaux d’entrée et [SOURCE: Page_68]

68 [SOURCE: Page_68]

1 introduction générale [SOURCE: Page_69]

de sortie mesurés sur un intervalle de temps passé doivent permettre de localiser l’état réel à l’intérieur d’une boule de rayon 𝜀 quelconque. Autrement dit : [SOURCE: Page_69]

(i) Soit 𝜀 > 0. Le système (Σ) est dit 𝜀-observateur pratique / 𝜀-practical observer (𝜀-PObs) pour un contrôle 𝑢, s’il existe un temps 𝛼 > 0 et une multi-application [SOURCE: Page_69]

Ω𝜀 : Y[𝑡−𝛼,𝑡] × U[𝑡−𝛼,𝑡] ⇒ X, [SOURCE: Page_69]

telle que, pour toute condition initiale 𝑥 0 ∈ X et toute perturbation 𝑣 ∈ V, on ait : [SOURCE: Page_69]

Ω𝜀 𝑦𝑥 0,𝑢,𝑣 ([𝑡 − 𝛼, 𝑡]), 𝑢 ([𝑡 − 𝛼, 𝑡]) ⊂ 𝐵𝜀 (𝑥 (𝑡)), ∀𝑡 ≥ 𝛼,  (1.79) [SOURCE: Page_69]

où 𝐵𝜀 (𝑥 (𝑡)) désigne la boule ouverte de rayon 𝜀 centrée en 𝑥 (𝑡). (ii) Le système (Σ) est dit Fortement pratiquement observable / Strongly practically observable (SPO) s’il est 𝜀-PO 𝜀-PO pour tout 𝜀 > 0, c’est-à-dire s’il existe, pour chaque 𝜀, un contrôle 𝑢𝜀 telle que la propriété 1.79 précédente soit satisfaite. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Pour tout 𝜀 > 0, on cherche l’erreur d’estimation dans la boule 𝐵𝜀 , en se basant unique- ment sur des entrées connues et des sorties mesurées du passé jusqu’à l’instant où l’on veut reconstituer 𝑥. Évidemment, même si c’est possible tout dépendra de la structure du système, de sa dépendance par rapport au vecteur de perturbation inconnu 𝑣. Lorsque la fonction 𝑓 dans le système (Σ) est composée de deux sous-fonctions 𝑓1 et 𝑓2 , et qu’une partie du vecteur d’état 𝑥 (𝑡), à savoir 𝑥 1 (𝑡), est accessible via la mesure 𝑦 (𝑡) = 𝑥 1 (𝑡), le problème consiste à reconstruire la composante non mesurée 𝑥 2 (𝑡). Plus précisément, dans le système suivant :  𝑥¤1 (𝑡) = 𝑓1 𝑥 1 (𝑡), 𝑥 2 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑣 (𝑡) ,  (1.80)                       𝑥¤2 (𝑡) = 𝑓2 𝑥 1 (𝑡), 𝑥 2 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑣 (𝑡) , 𝑦 (𝑡) = 𝑥 1 (𝑡), 𝑡 ≥ 0, [SOURCE: Page_69]

où 𝑥 1 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛1 , 𝑥 2 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛2 , avec 𝑛 = 𝑛 1 + 𝑛 2 , on cherche à reconstruire 𝑥 2 (𝑡) à partir de la relation :                                                          𝑦¤ (𝑡) = 𝑓1 𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑥 2 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑣 (𝑡) . [SOURCE: Page_69]

Cependant, la sortie mesurée 𝑦 (𝑡), sa dérivée 𝑦¤ (𝑡), ainsi que l’entrée 𝑢 (𝑡), ne suffisent généralement pas à garantir une reconstruction exacte de l’état 𝑥 2 (𝑡), notamment en présence de perturbations inconnues 𝑣 (𝑡). Les propriétés de la fonction 𝑓1 ainsi que la structure de l’ensemble des perturbations V jouent alors un rôle déterminant. Si la fonction 𝑓1 est inversible par rapport à 𝑥 2 et que cette inversion dépend unique- ment de 𝑦, 𝑦,¤ et 𝑢, alors le système est classiquement dit observable. En revanche, si l’inverse dépend également de la perturbation inconnue 𝑣, l’observabilité classique n’est [SOURCE: Page_69]

69 [SOURCE: Page_69]

1 introduction générale [SOURCE: Page_70]

plus garantie. Dans ce cas, une étude d’observabilité pratique peut permettre, selon la structure de V, de localiser l’erreur d’estimation de 𝑥 2 (𝑡) dans une boule centrée en un point défini par les historiques 𝑦 [𝑡−𝛼,𝑡] et 𝑢 [𝑡−𝛼,𝑡] , avec un rayon dépendant de V. Dans certains cas, la fonction 𝑓1 peut ne pas être globalement inversible. L’espace d’état X peut alors être partitionné en régions ouvertes X𝑖 , telles que, pour chaque point de X𝑖 , la fonction 𝑓1 possède 𝑖 inverses distincts. D’autres situations particulières sont discutées en détail dans Chapitre 4, notamment le cas où 𝑥 2 (𝑡) apparaît multipliée par un gain matriciel singulier, dépendant de variables connues, ce qui rend l’inversion possible uniquement sur un sous-espace de l’état. Bien sûr, les définitions de l’observabilité pratique dépendent explicitement du contrôle 𝑢, dans la mesure où les mauvaises entrées, peuvent empêcher la localisation de l’erreur d’estimation de l’état 𝑥 (𝑡) à l’intérieur de la boule de rayon 𝜀. Il est donc crucial de sélec- tionner les contrôles de manière appropriée pour satisfaire la propriété d’observabilité pratique. Naturellement, une fois la notion d’observabilité pratique établie, il est nécessaire d’introduire la notion correspondante d’observateur pratique. Il s’agit d’un estimateur qui, au lieu de garantir une reconstruction exacte de l’état, assure une convergence vers un voisinage borné arbitrairement petit de l’état réel. Plusieurs variantes de tels observateurs ont été proposées dans la littérature [Rapaport & Gouzé 2003]. Ces approches reformulent le problème d’estimation d’état en visant une erreur bornée acceptable, ce qui permet d’obtenir des performances robustes face aux incertitudes et perturbations [Awais et al. 2021; Zheng et al. 2016]. De telles méthodes permettent non seulement d’étendre le champ d’application de la conception d’observateurs à une plus grande diversité de systèmes, mais elles répondent également plus efficacement aux contraintes pratiques rencontrées dans les applications réelles. Pour une classe de systèmes dynamiques avec des termes non-linéaires incertains considérés comme des entrées inconnues, Rapaport & Gouzé (2003) montrent qu’il n’existe pas d’observateur exact indépendant des entrées inconnues. Ce qui est à l’origine de la notion d’observateur pratique faible et fort. Ainsi en s’adaptant à [Rapaport & Gouzé 2003], pour le système (Σ), si, pour tout 𝜀 > 0, il existe un système de la forme                          𝑧¤ (𝑡) = 𝑔 𝑧 (𝑡), 𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡) , 𝑥b(𝑡) = ℓ 𝑧 (𝑡), 𝑦 (𝑡) , [SOURCE: Page_70]

où 𝑧 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑟 (𝑟 ≥ 𝑛) représente l’état interne de l’observateur, et 𝑥b(𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 est l’estimation de l’état réel 𝑥 (𝑡) tel que : [SOURCE: Page_70]

∀𝑥 (0), 𝑧 (0), ∃𝑇 > 0 tel que ∥b 𝑥 (𝑡) − 𝑥 (𝑡)∥ ≤ 𝜀,     ∀𝑡 ≥ 𝑇 , [SOURCE: Page_70]

70 [SOURCE: Page_70]

1 introduction générale [SOURCE: Page_71]

alors cette famille d’estimateurs, paramétrée par 𝜀, constitue un observateur pratique faible. L’observateur n’assure donc pas une estimation exacte de l’état (où l’erreur d’estimation converge asymptotiquement vers zéro), mais garantit que, passé un certain temps 𝑇 , l’erreur d’estimation est inférieure à une tolérance donnée 𝜀. L’observateur pratique faible ne permet pas d’ajuster la vitesse de convergence vers la boule de rayon 𝜀. Pour garantir cela, le concept d’observateur pratique fort a été introduit. Un observateur pratique faible qui satisfait la propriété suivante : [SOURCE: Page_71]

∀𝜀 > 0, ∃𝜆𝜀 > 0, ∀𝑥 (0), 𝑧 (0), ∀𝑡 ≥ 0,      𝑥 (𝑡) − 𝑥 (𝑡)∥ ≤ 𝜀 + 𝑒 −𝜆𝜀 𝑡 (∥b ∥b                              𝑥 (0) − 𝑥 (0)∥ − 𝜀) , [SOURCE: Page_71]

avec 𝜆𝜀 → +∞ lorsque 𝜀 → 0 est appelé observateur pratique fort. Notons que ces définitions ont été introduites dans [Rapaport & Gouzé 2003] en absence de contrôle pour les systèmes soumis à des incertitudes. En nous appuyant sur la définition d’observabilité pratique, nous introduisons ci-dessous les concepts d’observateurs 𝜀-pratiques. Considérons une famille de systèmes dynamiques paramétrée par 𝜀 > 0 : [SOURCE: Page_71]

𝑧¤ (𝑡) = 𝑔𝜀 (𝑧 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑦 (𝑡)), (1.81) 𝑥b(𝑡) = ℓ𝜀 (𝑧 (𝑡), 𝑦 (𝑡)), 𝑡 ≥ 0, [SOURCE: Page_71]

où 𝑧 (𝑡) ∈ Z ⊂ ℝ𝑟 (pour un certain entier 𝑟 ) représente l’état interne de l’observateur, et 𝑥b(𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 est l’estimation de l’état du système. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Définition 1.21 (Observateurs 𝜀-pratiques). (i) Soit 𝜀 > 0. Le système (1.81) est appelé un 𝜀-PObs pour le système (4.1) associé au contrôle 𝑢𝜀 (·), s’il existe des fonctions 𝛽𝜀 ∈ KL, 𝛾𝜀 ∈ K, ainsi que des constantes 𝜆𝜀 > 0, 𝜇𝜀 > 0, telles que pour toute condition initiale (𝑥 0, 𝑧 0 ) ∈ X × Z et toute perturbation 𝑣 ∈ V, on ait :  𝑥 (𝑡) − 𝑥 (𝑡)∥ ≤ 𝜀 + 𝛽𝜀 (∥b ∥b                           𝑥 (0) − 𝑥 0 ∥, 𝜆𝜀 𝑡) + 𝛾𝜀 𝜇𝜀 ∥𝑣 ∥ [0,𝑡) , ∀𝑡 > 0. [SOURCE: Page_71]

(ii) Le système (1.81) est dit un Observateur pratique fort / Strong practical observer (SPObs) s’il est un observateur 𝜀-pratique pour tout 𝜀 > 0, avec 𝜆𝜀 → ∞ et 𝜇𝜀 → 0 lorsque 𝜀 → 0. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Dans le Chapitre 4, nous avons proposé un 𝜀-PObs pour une classe de système non- linéaires de la forme : [SOURCE: Page_71]

(1.82)       𝑥¤ (𝑡) = 𝐴𝑥 (𝑡) + 𝜑 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡)) + 𝐷 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡))𝜙 (𝐻𝑥 (𝑡)) + 𝑣 (𝑡), (1.83)       𝑦 (𝑡) = 𝐶𝑥 (𝑡), ∀𝑡 ≥ 0, [SOURCE: Page_72]

où 𝑥 (𝑡) ∈ X ⊂ ℝ𝑛 , 𝑢 (𝑡) ∈ U ⊂ ℝ𝑚 , 𝑦 (𝑡) ∈ Y ⊂ ℝ𝑝 , et 𝑣 (𝑡) ∈ V ⊂ ℝ𝑛 désignent respectivement l’état, le contrôle, l’observation et la perturbation. Les matrices 𝐴 ∈ ℝ𝑛×𝑛 , 𝐶 ∈ ℝ𝑝×𝑛 et 𝐻 ∈ ℝ𝑤×𝑛 sont données et connues, les fonctions 𝜑 : ℝ𝑝 × ℝ𝑚 → ℝ𝑛 , 𝜙 : ℝ𝑤 → ℝ𝑞 et 𝐷 : ℝ𝑝 × ℝ𝑚 → ℝ𝑛×𝑞 sont localement lipschitziennes (à valeurs vectorielles ou matricielles). La non-linéarité dépendante de l’état non mesuré est notée 𝜙 (𝐻𝑥 (𝑡)). En l’absence de non-linéarité, il est au moins nécessaire de garantir l’existence d’un observateur asymptotique pour le système (1.82)–(1.83). Cela justifie une hypothèse de détectabilité pour la paire (𝐴, 𝐶). Toutefois, cette hypothèse n’implique en rien l’observabilité du système en présence de 𝜙 (𝐻𝑥 (𝑡)). De manière générale, nous considérons le cas où le système (1.82)–(1.83) n’est pas observable. L’hypothèse principale de notre travail consiste alors à remplacer la non- linéarité qui rend le système non observable au sens classique par une fonction Φ(𝐻𝑥 (𝑡)) située à une distance 𝜀 1 de 𝜙 (𝐻𝑥 (𝑡)), de manière à garantir que le modèle (1.82)–(1.83) est 𝜀-PObs. Autrement dit, [SOURCE: Page_72]

Hypothèse 1.22. Il existe un réel 𝜀 1 > 0 tel que [SOURCE: Page_72]

∥𝜙 (𝐻𝑥) − Φ(𝐻𝑥)∥ ≤ 𝜀 1, ∀𝑥 ∈ X, [SOURCE: Page_72]

où la fonction Φ vérifie la contrainte quadratique suivante, pour tout 𝑠 1, 𝑠 2 ∈ ℝ𝑤 :                    ⊤                          𝑠1 − 𝑠2            𝐸   𝑅      𝑠1 − 𝑠2 ≥ −𝜀 2 Φ(𝑠 1 ) − Φ(𝑠 2 )     𝑅 ⊤ −2Υ Φ(𝑠 1 ) − Φ(𝑠 2 ) [SOURCE: Page_72]

pour certaines matrices 𝐸 ∈ S𝑤 , 𝑅 ∈ ℝ𝑤×𝑞 et un réel 𝜀 2 ≥ 0, où pour 𝜈 ∈ ℕ∗ , S𝜈 désigne l’ensemble des matrices carrées de taille 𝜈 symétriques définies positives. [SOURCE: Page_72]

Cela nécessite, a priori, la sélection d’un contrôle particulier qui assure la possibilité d’une estimation au sens pratique, c’est-à-dire, [SOURCE: Page_72]

Hypothèse 1.23. Étant donnée un contrôle 𝑢 : ℝ+ → U, il existe une matrice Υ ∈ S𝑞 telle que 𝐷 ⊤ (𝑦, 𝑢 (𝑡))𝐷 (𝑦, 𝑢 (𝑡)) ≤ Υ, ∀𝑦 ∈ Y, ∀𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ . [SOURCE: Page_72]

1.9 conclusion et perspectives [SOURCE: Page_74]

Les travaux de recherche présentés dans ce manuscrit constituent une étape clé dans le développement d’un outil d’aide à la décision pour la gestion de la fertirrigation en con- texte de pénurie d’eau, notamment grâce à l’intégration de stratégies de réutilisation. Nous avons proposé des stratégies optimales de fertirrigation basées sur un modèle de culture, une analyse de viabilité et des techniques de contrôle optimal. Ces stratégies visent à ajuster les apports en eau et en azote de manière à maximiser la production de biomasse, en tenant compte de la quantité initiale d’azote disponible dans le sol. Un observateur adaptatif robuste a été développé pour estimer conjointement cette quantité initiale ainsi que certains paramètres incertains du modèle. Nous avons introduit des notions d’observabilité, telles que 𝜀-observabilité pratique / 𝜀- practical observability (𝜀-Po) et Observabilité pratique forte / Strong practical observability (SPo), adaptées à des systèmes non linéaires soumis à des incertitudes bornées. Les preuves de convergence des différents types d’observateurs proposés s’appuient sur des techniques issues de la théorie de la stabilité de Lyapunov et de ses propriétés relatives. Cependant, le modèle de culture utilisé présente certaines limites. Il ne prend pas en compte l’excès d’azote non absorbé par les plantes, qui peut contaminer les sols et les nappes phréatiques. De plus, les variations climatiques, intrinsèquement incertaines, ne sont pas intégrées dans le modèle. Afin d’améliorer le réalisme et l’efficacité du cadre proposé, plusieurs pistes de recherche futures peuvent être envisagées : • reconsidérer le modèle avec un terme de drainage ; • minimiser les pertes d’azote liées à un excès d’irrigation ; • caractériser le noyau de viabilité et identifier les politiques optimales associées ; [SOURCE: Page_74]

74 [SOURCE: Page_74]

1 introduction générale [SOURCE: Page_75]

• intégrer des incertitudes climatiques à l’aide de scénarios probabilistes ; • appliquer des méthodes d’optimisation stochastique adaptées à ce contexte. En raison de la non-observabilité uniforme du modèle de fertirrigation en dehors de l’ensemble E, une extension de l’observateur pratique au cas non autonome constitue une perspective intéressante pour l’estimation pratique de l’azote disponible dans le sol en cas de stress hydrique ou azoté. Une autre piste de recherche consisterait à optimiser la borne supérieure de l’erreur d’estimation associée à l’observateur 𝜀-PObs. Plus précisément, 𝜀 dépend des paramètres 𝜀 1 , 𝜀 2 et 𝜆min (𝑃). Une perspective consiste à minimiser cette quantité par rapport à ces paramètres, afin d’améliorer la borne sur l’erreur. Enfin, une perspective majeure consisterait à utiliser les différentes trajectoires optimales d’azote comme références pour concevoir une stratégie de suivi adaptatif, permettant d’ajuster dynamiquement le système de traitement en fonction des besoins réels des plantes. [SOURCE: Page_75]

publications et plan du manuscrit [SOURCE: Page_75]

Le présent manuscrit est structuré autour des principales contributions développées au cours de ma thèse. Chaque chapitre correspond à un article publié dans une revue à comité de lecture ou a été présenté dans une conférence internationale. Le reste du manuscrit est organisé comme suit : [SOURCE: Page_75]

a) Le Chapitre 2 présente les stratégies de minimisation de l’eau sous contrainte de viabilité et correspond à l’article [Dadjo, Rapaport, Harmand, et al. 2025b] accepté pour publication dans Journal of Optimization Theory and Applications (JOTA) dont les travaux préliminaires ont été publiés dans les actes de la 22nd European Control Conference (ECC) (voir [Dadjo, Rapaport, Harmand, et al. 2024]). b) Le Chapitre 3 traite un observateur adaptatif robuste pour une classe de systèmes non linéaires non autonomes utilisé pour l’estimation de l’azote disponible dans le sol et les paramètres incertains. Ce travail correspond à l’article [Dadjo, Rapaport, Harmand, et al. 2025a] accepté pour publication dans International Journal of Control (IJC) dont les travaux préliminaires ont été publiés dans les actes de 62nd IEEE Conference on Decision and Control (CDC) (voir [Dadjo, Efimov, et al. 2023]). c) Le Chapitre 4 propose la notion d’observabilité et d’observateur pratique pour les systèmes non linéaires autonomes. Une version améliorée de ce travail est en cours pour une soumission à une revue et la version préliminaire dans [Dadjo, [SOURCE: Page_75]

2.1 introduction [SOURCE: Page_80]

In many regions of the world, and especially in the arid ones, agricultural production faces water scarcity, leading to difficulties in satisfying food population needs. Crops irrigation with treated wastewater, instead of fresh water, is a solution for preserving water resources. This practice increasingly attracts the interests of decision-makers. The underlying idea is to preserve, at the water treatment step, the nutrients that are beneficial for crop growth, notably nitrogen, so that no additional nutrients, which may spread and contaminate soils, have to be brought. In this regard, irrigation with reused water amounts to fertigation (i.e., irrigation with nutrients supplied in water). In the present work, we propose to address the problem of optimizing crop production via fertigation. Although many works about optimization with classical irrigation can be found in the literature [Sunantara & Ramírez 1997; Shani, Tsur & Zemel 2004; Schüze, de Paly & Shamir 2011; Lopes et al. 2016; Boumaza et al. 2021], and several commercial software are available on the market [Cheviron et al. 2016; Schütze & Schmitz 2010], the optimization of fertigation has been comparatively much less considered, apart some recent works [Neto et al. 2021; Kefi et al. 2023; Haddon, Rapaport, et al. 2023]. With classical irrigation that covers the crop’s water needs during the whole agricultural season, the soil’s nitrogen concentration can be diluted to the point that it no longer meets the crop demands if the initial nitrogen content is insufficient. This often causes farmers to supply nitrogen at the beginning of the season with the risk of spreading unnecessary quantities of nitrogen in soils. Bringing nitrogen with water appears to be a safe solution, provided that its concentration is appropriate and does not end up with too much nitrogen in the soil at the end of the season. Here, one also faces a dilemma between nitrogen dilution and water supply, which we will find in the optimization problem. In the former works [Neto et al. 2021; Kefi et al. 2023; Haddon, Rapaport, et al. 2023], numerical investigations have been conducted, [SOURCE: Page_80]

80 [SOURCE: Page_80]

2 synthesis of optimal control under state constraints for crop fertigation [SOURCE: Page_81]

but a mathematical analysis of optimal control under constraints has not yet been done, as we do here. In this chapter, we consider a crop model describing hydric and nitrogen stresses inspired by existing literature [Steduto, Hsiao, Raes, et al. 2009; Pelak, Revelli & Porporato 2017] and former works [Kalboussi, Roux, Boumaza, et al. 2019; Boumaza et al. 2021; Haddon, Rapaport, et al. 2023]. We assume here that the crop growth is not limited by other resources or nutrients (such as phosphorus). As reported in the literature, water and nitrogen are the common limiting factors of crop yield [Sinclair & Rufty 2012]. The originality compared to existing literature models is to consider explicitly in the model that nitrogen can be brought with irrigation, modeling the so-called fertigation. With the help of this model and control theoretical tools, the current research aims to grasp the right balance between water and nitrogen inputs to be reached for good growth performances. The nitrogen input can be made by classical fertilization, for instance, at seed time or with fertigation. Fertigation is typically used when soil nitrogen content is insufficient to guarantee maximal growth even when the supplied water avoids the hydric stress but can be compensated by the nitrogen in the irrigation water. However, when the nitrogen concentration in supplied water is too low, an initial input has to be considered. Therefore, we are also looking for the analysis of the model to be able to characterize situations for which initial nitrogen inputs are necessary or not. For farmers, biomass production at harvesting time is usually the main performance index, but that has to be balanced with water consumption. In this thesis, we consider that achieving the nominal production is a "hard constraint," and we consider the optimal control, which consists of minimizing the total amount of water (with fertigation) during a season under this constraint. To ensure this last problem is feasible with the features of the fertigation system, we consider the viability domain as the set of initial conditions, given by the moisture and nitrogen contents at seeding time, for which it is possible to obtain the maximal production at harvesting time. Our viability analysis allows us to characterize the operating parameters of the fertigation systems, which are nitrogen concentration in irrigation water and maximal irrigation rate for which the viability property is satisfied. The limits of the viability conditions that we have identified with this model provide the minimal inputs to obtain the maximal production at harvesting time, avoiding unnecessary overload of the soil with nitrogen content. In the second step, we study the optimal control problem with constraints, which consists of having the maximal production (that amounts to stay in the viability domain) while minimizing the total water consumption. This chapter is structured as follows. In the next section, we present the model and its hypotheses. In Section 2.3, we posit the viability problem and give conditions for the viability property to be fulfilled. In Section 2.4, we formulate the optimal control under [SOURCE: Page_81]

81 [SOURCE: Page_81]

2 synthesis of optimal control under state constraints for crop fertigation [SOURCE: Page_82]

constraints feasible under the former conditions. We also distinguish cases according to which constraint is saturated and in which order. In particular, we exploit a recent extension of the Bang-Bang Principle for piecewise affine dynamics [Chenevat et al. 2024] to define a family of control strategies. Section 2.5 illustrates our results and discusses several points related to non-intuitive situations and messages for practitioners. Finally, Section 2.6 summarizes our main results. [SOURCE: Page_82]

The growth function 𝑔 is 𝐶 1 and positive for non null biomass. The control 𝑢 is the irrigation rate, that takes values in a set U = [0, 𝑢 max ]. Parameters 𝑘𝑖 (𝑖 = 1, · · · , 4) are all positive numbers, where 𝑘 1 is a rate related to soil porosity, 𝑘 3 a stochiometric coefficient, and 𝑘 2 , 𝑘 4 are related to characteristics of the irrigation system. The originality of this model is to consider irrigation water containing nitrogen at concentration denoted 𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 , a practice known as fertigation. Note that adding nitrogen needed for crops in this manner requires adding water, which could require more water than needed. On the other hand, providing water needed for crops could dilute the nitrogen present in soil if the concentration 𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 is not large enough. This interplay between water and nitrogen supplies requires to investigate beneficial strategies for crops that consume reasonable amounts of water, which is the main purpose of the present chapter. We shall say that a control (time function) 𝑢 is admissible if it belongs to 𝐿 1 ([0,𝑇 ], 𝑈 ) and the corresponding solution 𝑆 remains on the domain [0, 1]. Clearly, any solution of (2.1) with a non-negative initial solution remains non-negative whatever is the control. The state constraint 𝑆 ≤ 1 can be simply satisfied by imposing the following condition on the control 1 {𝑆 = 1} ⇒ 𝑢 ≤       . 𝑘2 From the agronomic viewpoint, this means that when the soil is saturated, i.e. 𝑆 ≡ 1, the irrigation has to be regulated to avoid water loss. We shall denote U the set of admissible controls 𝑢. We shall say that the crop suffers from hydric stress when the value of the function 𝐾𝑆 is not maximal (that is when 𝑆 < 𝑆 ★), and nitrogen stress when the value of the function 𝑓 is not maximal (that is when 𝑁 − 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 < 0). The parameters 𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 and 𝑢 max are the operating parameters of the fertigation. This model does not consider climatic events like rain and droughts because we implicitly assume that crops grow in a greenhouse. [SOURCE: Page_83]

2.4 the optimal control problem [SOURCE: Page_87]

We assume in the rest of the chapter that the operating parameters 𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 , 𝑢 max verify the viability conditions: [SOURCE: Page_87]

𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 ≥ 𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 and 𝑢 max ≥ 𝑢 max, [SOURCE: Page_87]

where 𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 and 𝑢 max are given in (2.8)-(2.9). According to Proposition 2.3, the set 𝐸 defined in (2.7) is thus viable, and one can consider the optimization problem of minimizing the total amount of water delivered on [0,𝑇 ] while remaining in the set 𝐸, (i.e. guaranteeing the maximal biomass production at time 𝑇 ). [SOURCE: Page_87]

Problem P: For (𝑆 0, 𝑁 0 ) ∈ 𝐸, search ∫ 𝑇 inf       𝑢 (𝜏)𝑑𝜏 s.t. (𝑆 𝑢0,𝑆0 (𝑡), 𝑁 0,𝑆 𝑢 0 ,𝑁 0 (𝑡)) ∈ 𝐸, 𝑡 ∈ [0,𝑇 ]. 𝑢∈U    0 [SOURCE: Page_87]

Problem P takes the form of a standard optimal control problem with state constraints, and classical argumentation ensures the existence of optimal solutions (see, for instance, [Cesari 1983]). A usual approach to studying optimal solutions is to write necessary optimality conditions from the Maximum Principle with state constraints. In general, these conditions do not provide straightforwardly the optimal solution because one has to analyze the solutions of the adjoint system with the maximization condition of the Hamiltonian and terminal conditions. We failed to characterize the optimal solutions of our problem in this way due to the non-autonomous behavior of the dynamics (the Hamiltonian is no constant along optimal solutions) and the measure-multipliers of the state constraints (or jumps in the adjoint variables) to be determined, which make together the analysis quite intricate, even though our constraints are of relative degree 1 for which formulas are available [Schättler 2006]. We have chosen another route exploiting the particular structure of the dynamics and the constraints with a recent extension of the Bang-Bang Principle for piecewise affine systems [Chenevat et al. 2024]. As usual for optimal control problems linear in the control variable, we call "bang arc" a part of trajectory with constant control 𝑢 that takes extreme value (0 or 𝑢 max ) and "singular arc" a part of trajectory for which the Hamiltonian is singular in the control 𝑢 (i.e. for which the switching function is identically null). When dealing with constraints, we distinguish also "boundary arc" as a part of trajectory for which at least one constraint is saturated. [SOURCE: Page_87]

87 [SOURCE: Page_87]

T := {(𝑆, 𝑁 , 𝑀, 𝑉 , 𝑍 1, 𝑍 2, 𝑍 3 ) ∈ ℝ7+ ; (𝑆, 𝑁 ) ∈ 𝐸, 𝑀 = 𝑀 ★, 𝑉 = 𝑉 ★, 𝑍𝑖 = 0 (𝑖 = 1, 2, 3)} [SOURCE: Page_88]

from the initial condition (𝑆 0, 𝑁 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0) for system (2.15) with controls in U, and that the minimal time is exactly 𝑇 . Note that the dynamics (2.15) is (non-autonomous) piecewise affine with a convex control set U, and its non-differential locus is exactly on the boundary of 𝐸. On can then apply the result given in [Chenevat et al. 2024], which provides an extension of the well-known Bang-Bang Principle and states that a reachable target for a piecewise (non-autonomous) affine dynamics with a compact convex control set is reached in minimal time with a control that takes extreme values where the dynamics is smooth with possible singular arcs at non-differential locus. Clearly, the associated trajectory to such a control satisfy 𝑍𝑖 (𝑡) = 0 (𝑖 = 1, 2, 3) for 𝑡 ∈ [0,𝑇 ] and thus satisfy the constraint (𝑆 (𝑡), 𝑁 (𝑡)) ∈ 𝐸 for any 𝑡 ∈ [0,𝑇 ]. This shows the existence of an optimal control with bang arcs in the interior of 𝐸 and possibly boundary arcs on the boundary of 𝐸. [SOURCE: Page_88]

2 synthesis of optimal control under state constraints for crop fertigation [SOURCE: Page_89]

Note that this general result does not provide information about the number and the order of switches and boundary arcs. However, it guides us to look for particular structures of the optimal solutions, which we did by exploiting the cascade property of the dynamics, where the 𝑆-dynamics does not depend on the other variable 𝑁 (but the optimal control has to depend on both variables because of the constraints), and using comparison arguments with the introduction of intermediate variables. Our approach is as follows: we first consider the simplest solutions with a bang and boundary controls (i.e. with a minimal number of commutations) that saturate only one constraint among the three constraints 𝑆 ≤ 1, 𝑆 ≥ 𝑆 ★, 𝑁 ≥ 𝜂𝑐 𝑆 and study their optimality. In the second step, we search for more complicated trajectories (i.e. with more commutations) that can saturate more than one constraint by combining the former strategies. Finally, we cover all possible initial conditions in 𝐸. Let us begin with a preliminary result that will be useful in the following. [SOURCE: Page_89]

intuition that for high values of the initial nitrogen content 𝑁 0 one expects the nitrogen stress to be never met, we characterize first values of 𝑁 0 for which the optimal solution saturates the hydric constraint 𝑆 ≥ 𝑆 ★ only (Section 2.4.1). Then, we study cases for which the nitrogen constraint 𝑁 ≥ 𝜂𝑆 is saturated before the hydric one (Section 2.4.2). For lower values of 𝑁 0 , we expect cases for which the nitrogen constraint 𝑁 ≥ 𝜂𝑆 only is saturated, what we study in Section 2.4.3. However, we found a possible gap among these sets of values of 𝑁 0 , but showed that it corresponds to singular cases in the sense that a convex combination of two particular extreme solutions, which is a singular arc, is optimal (Section 2.4.4). Note that this does not contradict the result in [Chenevat et al. 2024] as this latter one does not guarantee the uniqueness of optimal trajectories. [SOURCE: Page_90]

2.4.3 saturation of the nitrogen stress only [SOURCE: Page_97]

The cases with 𝑁 0 ≥ 𝑁 0† have already been treated in Section 2.4.1 and Section 2.4.2, where it has been proved that applying the control 𝑢 = 0 until one of the constraints is saturated is optimal. For 𝑁 0 < 𝑁 0† , we found this strategy to be non longer optimal. Instead, we consider a radically different strategy, which consists of irrigating as much as possible since the beginning with a "bang-bang" or "bang-boundary-bang" control in such a way that the nitrogen constraint only is saturated strictly at final time 𝑇 (indeed, we found that saturating the constraint 𝑁 − 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 ≥ 0 on a time interval of positive length is not optimal if the trajectory does not touch 𝑆 = 𝑆𝑆★). This new strategy is defined as follows. [SOURCE: Page_97]

For 𝑡 > 𝑡𝑐 , the expression of the feedback (2.45) ensures that one has 𝑁 (𝑡) − 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 (𝑡) ≥ 0. The nitrogen constraint is thus satisfied with the 𝑁 𝑆 ★-strategy whatever is the commutation time 𝑡𝑐 . [SOURCE: Page_104]

Recall from Section 2.4.3 that for 𝑁 0 = 𝑁˜ 0♯ , the 𝑁 -strategy is optimal for a certain commu- tation time 𝑡𝑐♯ > 0. Denote (𝑆 ♯, 𝑁 ♯ ) the corresponding solution. For 𝑁 0 > 𝑁˜ 0♯ , notice that the control 𝑢 𝑁 (·, ·, 𝑡𝑐♯ ) remains admissible because the corresponding solution is exactly given by 𝑆 (𝑡) = 𝑆 ♯ (𝑡), 𝑁 (𝑡) = 𝑁 ♯ (𝑡) + 𝑁 0 − 𝑁˜ 0♯ > 𝑁 ♯ (𝑡) for any 𝑡 ∈ [0,𝑇 ]. Consequently, one has 𝑁 (𝑇 ) − 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 (𝑇 ) > 0. By continuity of the solution with respect to the commutation time 𝑡𝑐 , we deduce that there exists 𝜀 > 0 with 𝑡𝑐♯ − 𝜀 > 0 such that for any 𝑡𝑐 ∈ (𝑡𝑐♯ − 𝜀, 𝑡𝑐♯ ), the control 𝑢 𝑁 (·, ·, 𝑡𝑐♯ ) gives a solution with 𝑁 (𝑇 ) − 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 (𝑇 ) > 0. However, with 𝑡𝑐 < 𝑡𝑐♯ , the solution verifies 𝑆 (𝑇 ) < 𝑆 ♯ (𝑇 ) = 𝑆 ★. Finally, notice that for 𝑡𝑐 ∈ (𝑡𝑐♯ − 𝜀, 𝑡𝑐♯ ), the 𝑁 -strategy coincides with the the 𝑁 𝑆 ★ one. On the other hand, for 𝑡𝑐 = 0 the 𝑁 𝑆 ★-strategy coincides with the 𝑁 𝑆-strategy which is optimal for 𝑁 0 = 𝑁 0† . Denote (𝑆 †, 𝑁 † ) the corresponding solution. For 𝑁 0 < 𝑁 0† , the solution (𝑆, 𝑁 ) with 𝑡𝑐 = 0 meets the constraint 𝑁 = 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 before the solution (𝑆 †, 𝑁 † ) does, which implies that one has 𝑆 (𝑇 ) > 𝑆 ★ (and thus 𝑁 (𝑇 ) = 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 (𝑇 ) > 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 ★, i.e. 𝑡𝑐 = 0 cannot be optimal for 𝑁 0 ∈ ( 𝑁˜ 0♯, 𝑁 0† )). By continuity of the solution with respect to the commutation time, we deduce that there exists 𝑡ˆ𝑐 ∈ (0, 𝑡𝑐♯ −𝜀) such that the solution (𝑆, 𝑁 ) with the 𝑁 𝑆 ★-strategy verifies 𝑆 (𝑇 ) = 𝑆 ★. Finally, exploiting the results of Proposition 2.11, Proposition 2.14, Proposition 2.20 and Proposition 2.26, we refine the result of the former Proposition 2.5 as follows. [SOURCE: Page_105]

Theorem 2.28. For any initial condition (𝑆 0, 𝑁 0 ) ∈ 𝐸, Problem P admits an optimal solution which is composed of bang arcs in the interior of 𝐸 and possibly boundary arcs on the boundary of 𝐸, with up to three commutations. [SOURCE: Page_105]

Then, we have determined by solving numerically the differential equation (2.1) the various quantities considered in Proposition 2.11, Proposition 2.20 and Proposition 2.14, that are reported them in Table 2.3. From Table 2.3, one has 𝐶𝑆 < 0, and from Proposition 2.11 we get that the 𝑆-strategy is optimal for 𝑁 0 ≥ 𝑁 0♭ . In Table 2.3, we see also that one has 𝑁 0♯ > 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 0 , and then the 𝑁 -strategy is optimal for 𝑁 0 ∈ [𝜂𝐶 𝑆 0, 𝑁 0♯ ] according to 2.20. For various values of 𝑁 0 , we have determined numerically the optimal solutions with the help of the Bocop software, which is based on a direct method [Team Commands 2017]. We can see in Figure 2.2a to Figure 2.4b that the solutions are in perfect accordance with the theoretical results, as predicted by Table 2.4. Of course, the advantage of the analytical analysis is that it provides feedback strategies instead of open loops given by the numerical method, which is easier and more robust to apply in practice. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Let us comment on these optimal solutions. [SOURCE: Page_109]

• When the initial quantity of nitrogen is high (𝑁 0 > 𝑁 0♭ ), it is not surprising that the nitrogen constraint is not saturated. Indeed, the 𝑆-strategy coincides with the optimal one already found in the literature for the simplified model with no nitrogen stress [Boumaza et al. 2021]. It consists of no irrigation until the humidity threshold 𝑆 ★ is met and then keeping the humidity level equal to this threshold up to the final time. A sensor measuring the humidity level 𝑆 is required to apply this control strategy. • For the lower value of initial nitrogen 𝑁 0 ∈ (𝑁 0†, 𝑁 0♭ ), the lack of irrigation causes the system to face the nitrogen stress before the hydric one. Then, the [SOURCE: Page_109]

109 [SOURCE: Page_109]

2 synthesis of optimal control under state constraints for crop fertigation [SOURCE: Page_110]

optimal solution consists of keeping the system at the edge of this constraint until it reaches the humidity threshold and then keeping the humidity level precisely equal to this threshold (as for the 𝑆-strategy). In this way, the 𝑁 𝑆- strategy generalizes the 𝑆-strategy in the context of nitrogen stress. Here, as online information, the measurement of the nitrogen content 𝑁 (or its concentration 𝑁 /𝑆) is required, in addition to the humidity level 𝑆. • A surprising feature occurs when keeping the system at the edge of the nitrogen stress does not allow it to reach the humidity threshold (because the time horizon has been reached before) for small initial nitrogen content 𝑁 0 < 𝑁 0♯ . Then, the 𝑁 𝑆-strategy is no longer optimal, and the best one is radically different. The 𝑁 - strategy consists of irrigating since the beginning to maintain the humidity level at its maximal level up to a precise time 𝑡𝑐★, from which stopping the irrigation conducts the system to touch the 𝑁 -stress precisely at the terminal time. Unlike the 𝑆-strategy, the 𝑁 -strategy is not a particular instance of the 𝑁 𝑆-strategy. On the contrary, the 𝑁 -strategy requires anticipating the future and providing water from the beginning. From a practical viewpoint, this "open-loop" strategy requires the precise determination of the optimal commutation time 𝑡𝑐★ from the model’s data an initial 𝑁 0 , without the need for future online measurement. • Another non-intuitive feature is the possibility for a subset of initial conditions 𝑁 0 ∈ (𝑁 0♯, 𝑁 0† ) to reach the "corner" point (𝑆 ★, 𝜂𝐶 𝑆 ★), defined as the intersection of the two constraints, precisely at the final time, and different strategies are possible. The 𝑁 𝑆 ★-strategy does not require irrigation on a time window, but differently to the other cases, this time window is not at the beginning or the end of the season. Other possibilities are singular controls given by particular irrigation strategies, which no longer consist of keeping the system at boundaries of the set 𝐸 or without irrigation. Such strategies are most demanding to implement because they require the prior determination of the whole time profile depending on the initial 𝑁 0 . However, it can obtained with smooth controls but remains open-loops. For these cases, the final nitrogen content is the smallest among all possible ones. [SOURCE: Page_110]

On Figure 2.6, we have plotted the optimal value of the water consumption 𝑉 and the corresponding final nitrogen 𝑁 (𝑡), as function of the initial nitrogen content. [SOURCE: Page_110]

If one intends to minimize both the water consumption and the residual nitrogen content (while ensuring maximal biomass production), a Pareto diagram might be helpful. In Figure 2.7, one can see that the Pareto front is for 𝑁 0 ∈ [𝑁 0†, 𝑁 0♭ ], for which a compromise between water consumption and final nitrogen content has to be chosen. Note that this locus corresponds to the optimality of the 𝑁 𝑆-strategy. [SOURCE: Page_111]

2.6 conclusion [SOURCE: Page_115]

Considering the problem of minimal irrigation under maximal biomass production for a simplified crop model, we have provided conditions for this optimal control problem with [SOURCE: Page_115]

115 [SOURCE: Page_115]

2 synthesis of optimal control under state constraints for crop fertigation [SOURCE: Page_116]

state constraints to be feasible, and given the different structures of the optimal strategy depending on the initial nitrogen content. We have characterized optimal solutions as concatenations of bang and boundary arcs, these latter ones being on the boundary of the constraints set only, and shown that they have at most three commutations. This analysis illustrates the extended Bang-Bang Principle for non-autonomous piecewise affine dynamics. We have also exhibited cases for which there exists an infinity of optimal singular trajectories, related to the non-smooth feature of the constraints set. These strategies can be implemented as simple feedback measuring online the humidity level 𝑆 if the limit of water stress is met before the harvesting time or the nitrogen concentration 𝑁 /𝑆 if the limit of the nitrogen stress is met before the harvesting time. In the other cases, both quantities need to be measured. We have also exhibited a particular set of initial conditions that conducts to the smallest residual nitrogen in the soil, which could serve as a theoretical target for the practitioners. This study revealed that when the crop radiation efficiency increases rapidly with time, and the nitrogen concentration in the irrigation water is low, then the minimal residual nitrogen content in the soil is obtained when the initial nitrogen content belongs to a particular interval of values, and not for the smallest initial nitrogen content. Therefore, having a low initial nitrogen content in the soil could lead paradoxically to larger residual contents. This can be explained by the larger water consumption needed to maintain maximal production. This non-intuitive feature is due to the interplay between nitrogen dilution and water supply in fertigation. This interval for the initial nitrogen content could serve as a theoretical target for practitioners. However, the practical application of the fertigation strategies would have to face some technical and sanitary issues (such as time-varying fluctuations, pipe clogging, and undesirable nano-particles or pathogens), that are not addressed in the model. Future perspectives of this work could concern non-viable cases, looking then to via- bility kernels, that are the largest subsets of initial conditions for which the optimal control problem under constraint is feasible [Aubin 2009], and the consideration of non deterministic climatic disturbances (rains, droughts). [SOURCE: Page_116]

3.1 introduction [SOURCE: Page_118]

Monitoring and supervising controlled systems often requires complete knowledge of the state vector at any time. However, in many applications, the available sensors cannot provide real-time access to the full state of the system for many reasons, such as economic factors (sensors are too expensive) or technical constraints (noisy environment or lack of reliable technology, for instance, nitrogen measurements in agriculture). Since only a part of the state vector (called the observation or output) is measured, the goal of an observer is to estimate the other part of the state vector that is not available with measurements. Historically, Kalman first tackled this problem in a linear stochastic framework, leading to the so-called Kalman filter [Kalman 1960], and Luenberger solved it for the case of deterministic linear systems [Luenberger 1971a]. Most physical systems are described by nonlinear models, and there is no systematic method for constructing observers in a general nonlinear setting. As examples of recent advances, in [Hernández- González et al. 2021], an observer was designed for a class of non-autonomous, state- affine, uncertain nonlinear systems with sampled output measurements based on a high-gain approach, and a novel chain observer was designed for a class of Lipschitz nonlinear systems in [Targui et al. 2022]. Observer for dynamic systems remains a central challenge in control theory, especially when system dynamics are nonlinear and parameters are partially unknown. The estimator design problem becomes even more difficult for time-dependent dynamics or in the presence of exogenous perturbations. In this context, observers for particular [SOURCE: Page_118]

3 a robust adaptive observer for crop fertigation model [SOURCE: Page_119]

classes of nonlinear models have been proposed and developed to estimate the state and the parameters simultaneously in real time, coping with some uncertainty in the model. Such observers are called adaptive, and many contributions are available in the literature; see, for instance, [Annaswamy & Fradkov 2021; Besançon 2007; Marino & Tomei 1995; Young Man Cho & Rajamani 1997]. However, most of these approaches either assume complete knowledge of the system parameters or are limited to time-invariant systems, and rarely investigate the effect of perturbations. The problem of simultaneous reconstruction of unmeasured state variables and un- known parameters, with both disturbances and measurement perturbations for a class of nonlinear time-varying systems, is considered in this work. For this purpose, adap- tive observers have already been proposed in the literature in linear and nonlinear frameworks, starting from earlier works [Lüders & Narendra 1973; Marino & Tomei 1992], where two main families of design techniques have been developed when the unknown parameters appear in the derivative of the output, or when it appears first in higher order derivatives, this later case requires additional filters to compensate the high relative degree obstruction. Significant results have been formulated in the last thirty years, e.g. [Besançon 2000; Zhang 2002], where canonical forms of systems admitting nonlinear adaptive observers were defined, and the canonical structure of the auxiliary filters was given. In particular, a conceptual approach to the synthesis of adaptive state observers is proposed in [Zhang 2002] for linear time-varying or state-affine nonlinear systems (in the former case, the regressor is a time-varying matrix of known signals, whereas in the latter case, the regressor can depend on the input and output). The core idea is to incorporate an additional filter and an auxiliary term that is time-varying and proportional to the time derivative of the estimated parameter vector. Then, under persistent excitation, this approach ensures exponential convergence of both state and parameter estimation errors to zero. It is worth highlighting that an important restric- tion for a majority of these results is the requirement of dependence of the regressor (i.e., the gain function that multiplies the vector of unknown parameters) on measured input and output signals only, which was solved in [Farza et al. 2009] for the cases of linear and nonlinear parameterization. The system considered in [Farza et al. 2009] is a specific class of time-invariant nonlinear systems in which the regressor depends on a part of the unmeasured state. The proposed adaptive observer is primarily based on the high-gain approach from [Gauthier & Kupka 2004], as well as the approach of [Zhang 2002], where the corrective term is divided into two components: one using high-gain state observers and the other updating the unknown parameters as described in [Zhang 2002]. To the best of our knowledge, the non-autonomous nonlinear dynamics and the noisy measurements have not been addressed so far in the literature, even though many practical adaptive observer problems exhibit these features. Other challenges are the need to analyze the estimation error in the presence of state disturbances and measurement noises, where only special cases were studied [Efimov & Fradkov 2015b], [SOURCE: Page_119]

119 [SOURCE: Page_119]

3 a robust adaptive observer for crop fertigation model [SOURCE: Page_120]

without extensions to time-varying systems. Motivated by the above considerations, the present work makes the following key contributions. A new adaptive observer for a class of non-autonomous nonlinear dynamics with un- known inputs is proposed, for which the regressor depends not only on the known input-output signals but also on the unmeasured state, and the measurements are noisy. The analysis uses the theory of input-to-state stability (ISS) and other related properties. A Lyapunov function is synthesized to prove that the error dynamics is state-independent input-to-output stable from unknown inputs, relying on a condition of persistent excitation of the control variables. Under some constraints, these condi- tions are formulated in terms of linear matrix inequalities (LMIs). This work has been motivated by a concrete problem of crop irrigation. In the Chapitre 2, we addressed the problem of minimizing the amount of water delivered during a season, while satisfying the viability constraint. Usually, soil humidity and crop biomass can be measured or estimated online [Karam et al. 2019]. However, soil nitrogen content knowledge is often much more challenging, while it is crucial for growth prediction [Steduto, Hsiao, Raes, et al. 2009]. These variables’ dynamics are nonlinear, time-varying, and rely on several parameters that must be estimated for each soil-crop system [Brisson, Gary, Justes, Roche, Mary, Ripoche, Zimmer, Sierra, Bertuzzi, Burger, et al. 2003a]. Moreover, the available measurements are often noisy, and the state dynamics contain uncertain external inputs. We show here how our adaptive estimation approach applies to a crop fertigation model, which falls outside the scope of previous works due to its unique characteristics. To rigorously reconstruct unavailable nitrogen, preliminary research was conducted in [Dadjo, Efimov, et al. 2023]. The proofs of results presented in the latter are elaborated in this work for an enhanced model to address agronomic signifi- cance. Comparisons with the high-gain approach used in [Farza et al. 2009] prove that our proposed adaptive observer offers better robustness. The chapter is organized as follows. Section 4.2 introduces the preliminary concepts and notations. The considered class of nonlinear systems and the general problem formulation are presented in Section 3.3. The proposed adaptive observer is developed in Section 3.4, which is divided into three subsections. Section 3.4.1 describes the structure of the observer. Section 3.4.2 addresses the ISS-Lyapunov stability analysis of the estimation errors. Section 3.4.3 shows how the observer design problem can be recast as an LMI feasibility problem. Section 3.5 discusses a crop irrigation model, while Section 3.5.1 focuses on synthesizing an adaptive observer tailored for crop fertigation, aiming at reconstructing both unknown constant parameters and nitrogen content. Simulation results and performance comparisons with other approaches, conducted on a fertigation application to illustrate the robustness of our method, are presented in Section 3.6, and concluding remarks are given in Section 3.7. [SOURCE: Page_120]

120 [SOURCE: Page_120]

3 a robust adaptive observer for crop fertigation model [SOURCE: Page_121]

3.2 preliminaries [SOURCE: Page_121]

The following notation is used throughout the chapter. [SOURCE: Page_121]

• ℝ+ denotes the set of non-negative real numbers, ℝ𝑝 and ℝ𝑛×𝑚 denote the vector space of real vectors of dimension 𝑝 and the set of matrices with real coefficients of dimension 𝑛 × 𝑚, respectively. • 𝐼𝑛 denotes the identity matrix of dimension 𝑛 × 𝑛. • ∥ · ∥ denotes the Euclidean norm for vectors and the induced norm for matrices. • For a Lebesgue measurable function 𝑢 : ℝ+ → ℝ𝑚 , we define the norm ∥𝑢 ∥ [𝑡1,𝑡2 ) := ess sup𝑡 ∈[𝑡1,𝑡2 ) ∥𝑢 (𝑡)∥ for [𝑡 1, 𝑡 2 ) ⊂ ℝ+ . We denote by L𝑚 ∞ the set of functions 𝑢 with ∥𝑢 ∥ ∞ :=∥𝑢 ∥ [0,∞) < +∞. • A continuous function 𝜎 : ℝ+ → ℝ+ belongs to class K if it is strictly increasing and 𝜎 (0) = 0; it belongs to class K∞ if it is also radially unbounded. A continuous function 𝛽 : ℝ+ × ℝ+ → ℝ+ belongs to class KL if 𝛽 (·, 𝑠) ∈ K for all 𝑠 ∈ ℝ+ and 𝛽 (𝑟, ·) is a decreasing function with 𝛽 (𝑟, 𝑠) → 0 as 𝑠 → +∞ for any 𝑟 > 0. • 𝐴⊤ (resp. 𝜉 ⊤ ) denotes the transpose of the matrix 𝐴 (resp. the vector 𝜉). • For a matrix 𝑀 of ℝ𝑛×𝑛 , 𝑀 ⪯ 0 (resp. 𝑀 ≺ 0) means 𝜉 ⊤ 𝑀𝜉 ≤ 0 (resp. 𝜉 ⊤ 𝑀𝜉 < 0) for all 𝜉 ∈ ℝ𝑛 . • diag(𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐) denotes the diagonal matrix with elements 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐 on the main diagonal.                     ⊤ • For a matrix 𝑀 of ℝ𝑛×𝑚 , 𝑀1𝑗 𝑀2𝑗 . . . 𝑀𝑛 𝑗 ∈ ℝ𝑛×1 , 𝑗 = 1, . . . 𝑚 denotes the 𝑗th column of 𝑀. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Following SON, we consider a class of nonlinear systems: [SOURCE: Page_121]

𝑥¤ (𝑡) = 𝑓 (𝑥 (𝑡), 𝑑 (𝑡)), 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+, (3.1) 𝑦 (𝑡) = ℎ(𝑥 (𝑡)), [SOURCE: Page_121]

where 𝑥 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 is the state vector, 𝑑 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑚 is the external perturbation, with 𝑑 ∈ L𝑚∞, and 𝑦 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑝 the output vector. Moreover, 𝑓 : ℝ𝑛 × ℝ𝑚 → ℝ𝑛 is a locally Lipschitz continuous function with 𝑓 (0, 0) = 0, and ℎ : ℝ𝑛 → ℝ𝑝 is a continuously differentiable function. For an initial state 𝑥 0 ∈ ℝ𝑛 and 𝑑 ∈ L𝑚 ∞ , we denote the corresponding solution of the system (3.1) by 𝑥 (𝑡, 𝑥 0, 𝑑) for the values of 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ for which the solution exists. The corresponding output is denoted is 𝑦 (𝑡, 𝑥 0, 𝑑) = ℎ (𝑥 (𝑡, 𝑥 0, 𝑑)). [SOURCE: Page_121]

The definitions of stability concepts used in the chapter are given below. [SOURCE: Page_121]

121 [SOURCE: Page_121]

3 a robust adaptive observer for crop fertigation model [SOURCE: Page_122]

Definition 3.1. The system (3.1) is called forward complete if for all 𝑥 0 ∈ ℝ𝑛 and 𝑑 ∈ L𝑚 ∞, the solution 𝑥 (𝑡, 𝑥 0, 𝑑) is uniquely defined for all 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ . [SOURCE: Page_122]

Definition 3.2. A forward complete system (3.1) is: (i) practical input-to-output stable (pIOS) if there exist 𝛽 ∈ KL, 𝛾 ∈ K and 𝑐 ∈ ℝ+ such that ∥𝑦 (𝑡, 𝑥 0, 𝑑)∥ ≤ 𝛽 (∥𝑥 0 ∥, 𝑡) + 𝛾 (∥𝑑 ∥ ∞ ) + 𝑐, ∀𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ for any 𝑥 0 ∈ ℝ𝑛 and 𝑑 ∈ L𝑚∞ . The system is called input-to-output stable (IOS) if 𝑐 = 0. In the special case when 𝑦 = 𝑥, the IOS property is called ISS. (ii) state-independent input-to-output stable (SIIOS) if there exist 𝛽 ∈ KL and 𝛾 ∈ K such that ∥𝑦 (𝑡, 𝑥 0, 𝑑)∥ ≤ 𝛽 (∥ℎ(𝑥 0 )∥, 𝑡) + 𝛾 (∥𝑑 ∥ ∞ ), ∀𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ for any 𝑥 0 ∈ ℝ𝑛 and 𝑑 ∈ L𝑚 ∞. [SOURCE: Page_122]

One can deduce from this definition that SIIOS is a direct extension of the ISS property to systems providing convergence only for a part of the variables. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Definition 3.3. A forward complete system (3.1) is said to be uniformly bounded-input- bounded-state stable (UBIBS) if there exists 𝜎 ∈ K such that [SOURCE: Page_122]

∥𝑥 (𝑡, 𝑥 0, 𝑑)∥ ≤ max{𝜎 (∥𝑥 0 ∥), 𝜎 (∥𝑑 ∥ ∞ )}, ∀𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ for all 𝑥 0 ∈ ℝ𝑛 𝑎𝑛𝑑𝑑 ∈ L𝑚 ∞. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Definition 3.4. For the system (3.1), a smooth function 𝑉 : ℝ𝑛 → ℝ+ is: [SOURCE: Page_122]

(i) An IOS-Lyapunov function if there exist 𝛼 1, 𝛼 2 ∈ K∞ , 𝜒 ∈ K, and 𝛼 3 ∈ KL such that: [SOURCE: Page_122]

𝛼 1 (∥ℎ(𝑥)∥) ≤ 𝑉 (𝑥) ≤ 𝛼 2 (∥𝑥 ∥), 𝑉 (𝑥) ≥ 𝜒 (∥𝑑 ∥) ⇒ ∇𝑉 (𝑥)𝑓 (𝑥, 𝑑) ≤ −𝛼 3 (𝑉 (𝑥), ∥𝑥 ∥), ∀𝑥 ∈ ℝ𝑛 , 𝑑 ∈ ℝ𝑚 . [SOURCE: Page_122]

(ii) A SIIOS-Lyapunov function if there exist 𝛼 1, 𝛼 2 ∈ K∞ , and 𝜒, 𝛼 3 ∈ K such that: [SOURCE: Page_122]

𝛼 1 (∥ℎ(𝑥)∥) ≤ 𝑉 (𝑥) ≤ 𝛼 2 (∥ℎ(𝑥)∥), 𝑉 (𝑥) ≥ 𝜒 (∥𝑑 ∥) ⇒ ∇𝑉 (𝑥) 𝑓 (𝑥, 𝑑) ≤ −𝛼 3 (𝑉 (𝑥)), ∀𝑥 ∈ ℝ𝑛 , 𝑑 ∈ ℝ𝑚 . [SOURCE: Page_122]

Let us recall from the literature, the classical result: [SOURCE: Page_122]

Theorem 3.5. [Sontag 2001] A UBIBS (forward complete) system (3.1) is IOS (SIIOS) if and only if it admits an IOS (SIIOS)-Lyapunov function. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Recall the concept of persistent excitation used in stability of non-autonomous linear systems: [SOURCE: Page_123]

Definition 3.6. Let 𝜙 : ℝ+ → ℝ𝑚×𝑛 be a continuous bounded function. We say that 𝜙 is persistently exciting (PE) if there exists 𝛼 > 𝛼 > 0, 𝑇 ∗ > 0 such that ∫ 𝑡+𝑇 ∗ 𝛼𝐼𝑚 ≥           𝜙 (𝑠)𝜙 (𝑠) ⊤𝑑𝑠 ≥ 𝛼𝐼𝑚 , ∀𝑡 ≥ 0. 𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_123]

3.3 problem statement [SOURCE: Page_123]

Consider a class of nonlinear systems of the following form, for 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ : [SOURCE: Page_123]

𝑥¤ (𝑡) = 𝐴(𝑡)𝑥 (𝑡) + 𝜑 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡)) + 𝐺 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑥 (𝑡))𝜃 + 𝑑 (𝑡), (3.2)          𝑦 (𝑡) = 𝐶𝑥 (𝑡) + 𝑣 (𝑡), [SOURCE: Page_123]

where 𝑥 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 is the state, 𝑦 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑝 is the output measurement vector, 𝜃 ∈ ℝ𝑚 is the vector of unknown parameters, 𝑢 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑑 is the known input vector, 𝑣 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑝 is a measurement noise, 𝑑 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 is a disturbance. The matrix function 𝐴 : ℝ+ → ℝ𝑛×𝑛 and the matrix 𝐶 ∈ ℝ𝑛×𝑛 are given; the functions 𝐺 : ℝ𝑛+𝑝+𝑑 → ℝ𝑛×𝑚 and 𝜑 : ℝ𝑝+𝑑 → ℝ𝑛 are assumed to be continuous and guaranteeing existence of the solutions in forward time. For a time-varying matrix 𝐶, the procedure is similar to below, provided that it admits a uniform upper bound. Without loss of generality, we assume 𝑝 < 𝑛, which implies 𝑛 − 𝑝 states of the system (3.2) are non measured. In [Zhang 2002], the proposed system is linear with respect to the unmeasured state, and the regressor uniquely depends on the input and the measurable output. The novelty of our work lies in the fact that in system (3.2), the regressor can depend on the unmeasured state, and the system is nonlinear with respect to the full state. The boundedness of the regressor is no longer sufficient to guarantee the adaptive observer. The proof of the boundedness of the auxiliary variable in (3.3) is non-trivial, as the authors in [Zhang 2002] conclude that the global and exponential convergence of the state estimation error holds. A rigorous proof is provided in Proposition 3.14 in Section 3.4. [SOURCE: Page_123]

We shall require the following hypotheses. [SOURCE: Page_123]

𝑝 Assumption 3.7. 𝑑 ∈ L∞ 𝑛 , 𝑣 ∈ L , and ∥𝜃 ∥ ≤ 𝜃 ∞               max with a known bound 𝜃 max > 0. [SOURCE: Page_123]

Hence, the unknown inputs are bounded without a known upper limit, and the set of admissible values for the vector of unknown parameters is given. The latter is not a hard restriction since the parameters usually must respect physical constraints. [SOURCE: Page_123]

123 [SOURCE: Page_123]

3 a robust adaptive observer for crop fertigation model [SOURCE: Page_124]

Assumption 3.8. There exists 𝜆 > 0 such that ∥𝐺 (𝑦, 𝑢, 𝑥) − 𝐺 (𝑦, 𝑢, 𝑥) ˆ ∥ ≤ 𝜆∥𝑥 − 𝑥ˆ ∥, ∀𝑦 ∈ ℝ𝑝 , 𝑢 ∈ ℝ𝑑 and 𝑥, 𝑥ˆ ∈ ℝ𝑛 . [SOURCE: Page_124]

Assumption 3.9. There exists 𝐺 max > 0 such that ∥𝐺 (𝑦, 𝑢, 𝑥)∥ ≤ 𝐺 max , ∀𝑦 ∈ ℝ𝑝 , 𝑢 ∈ ℝ𝑑 and 𝑥 ∈ ℝ𝑛 . [SOURCE: Page_124]

Note these hypotheses imply that 𝐺 is uniformly Lipschitz continuous in the third argument and globally bounded. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Remark 3.10. The dependence on time can be introduced to the maps 𝜑 and 𝐺, which we have not done here for simplicity. Indeed, one can simply consider that the time 𝑡 is a measured variable of the system, adding the dynamics 𝑥¤𝑛+1 = 1, and a component of the observation vector 𝑦𝑝+1 (𝑡) = 𝑥𝑛+1 (𝑡) (without noise i.e. with 𝑣𝑛+1 (𝑡) = 0). [SOURCE: Page_124]

3.4 main result [SOURCE: Page_124]

In this section, we propose an adaptive observer for the class of nonlinear systems (3.2). The convergence of the estimation error is provided, and the respective conditions are reduced to the satisfaction of some LMIs. [SOURCE: Page_124]

3.4.1 adaptive observer equations [SOURCE: Page_124]

Following [Zhang 2002], we propose an observer in the form: [SOURCE: Page_124]

𝑥¤̂ (𝑡) = 𝐴(𝑡)𝑥ˆ (𝑡) + 𝜑 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡)) + 𝐺 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑥ˆ (𝑡))𝜃ˆ(𝑡) ¤̂ + 𝐿(𝑡)(𝑦 (𝑡) − 𝐶 𝑥ˆ (𝑡)) + Ω(𝑡)𝜃 (𝑡), (3.3) Ω(𝑡) ¤       = (𝐴(𝑡) − 𝐿(𝑡)𝐶)Ω(𝑡) + 𝐺 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑥ˆ (𝑡)), ¤̂ 𝜃 (𝑡) = 𝛾 Ω ⊤ (𝑡)𝐶 ⊤ (𝑦 (𝑡) − 𝐶 𝑥ˆ (𝑡)) [SOURCE: Page_124]

where 𝑥ˆ (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 is the estimate vector for 𝑥 (𝑡), 𝐿(𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛×𝑝 is the observer gain providing the desired stability property for the matrix 𝐴(𝑡) − 𝐿(𝑡)𝐶, Ω(𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛×𝑚 is an intermediate filter state variable, and 𝛾 = diag(𝛾 1, . . . , 𝛾𝑚 ), 𝛾𝑖 > 0, 𝑖 ∈ {1, . . . , 𝑚} is the adaptation gain. We detail other conditions below. Also, when a variable’s dependence on time is not ambiguous, it is omitted for brevity of presentation. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Next, by introducing an auxiliary estimation error and the parameter estimation error 𝛿 = 𝑥 − 𝑥ˆ − Ω(𝜃 − 𝜃ˆ), 𝜃˜ = 𝜃 − 𝜃ˆ and one obtains, after straightforward computations [SOURCE: Page_125]

𝛿¤ = (𝐴 − 𝐿𝐶)𝛿 + (𝐺 − 𝐺)𝜃 ˆ − 𝐿𝑣 + 𝑑, ¤̃ 𝜃 = −𝛾 Ω⊤𝐶 ⊤ (𝐶𝛿 + 𝐶 Ω𝜃˜ + 𝑣), [SOURCE: Page_125]

3.4.2 stability analysis [SOURCE: Page_125]

We now set 𝑒 := 𝑥 − 𝑥ˆ as the state estimation error. We aim to prove that 𝑒 and 𝜃˜ converge to 0 in the absence of the disturbances 𝑑 and 𝑣, and they stay bounded in the presence of bounded perturbations. Hence, we want to demonstrate the SIIOS property for inputs 𝑑 and 𝑣 considering 𝑒 and 𝜃˜ as outputs. [SOURCE: Page_125]

In this theorem, it is assumed that the gain function 𝐿 is chosen to ensure the stability of the time-varying matrix 𝐴 − 𝐿𝐶, which is equivalent to the existence of a positive definite matrix 𝑃 1 . Then, a positive definite matrix 𝑃2 exists. Unfortunately, there is no approach for generating a control input that guarantees the persistent excitation condition, and this remains an open problem in adaptive control theory. Note that the proposed observer can be further extended to get a better robustness or a faster convergence by adapting the existing results [Franco et al. 2021; Ríos et al. 2024]. The presented results have rather sophisticated applicability conditions, but we are going to show next that they can be easily verified in the application of interest. [SOURCE: Page_129]

3.5 application on a crop fertigation model with soil water drainage [SOURCE: Page_129]

A fundamental concern in agriculture is related to the state of the soil, which ensures good conditions for crop growth (such as tomatoes or lettuces), especially under climate change and drought events. The crop (or biomass) growth dynamic is a function of the water and nutrients in the soil. Crop models are used to describe crops’ growth during all seasons and for reliable predictions and production stimulation. Soil humidity is a crucial state variable in most crop models, and therefore, humidity sensors are widely used to evaluate this quantity over time. However, other information necessary to obtain an accurate prediction is contained in the parameters and in the soil nitrogen concentration [Klar et al. 2008; Fernández-Escobar et al. 2012]. Most of the time, sensors [SOURCE: Page_129]

3 a robust adaptive observer for crop fertigation model [SOURCE: Page_130]

of nitrogen content in soils are not available on the field, or inaccurate, or are too costly. This is where software sensors (observers) enter the picture as an expected alternative. Moreover, the reconstruction of the state of the crop in the field is also required for water management, which is one of the most critical production factors in agriculture. Water dynamics is a balance between the water brought from rain or irrigation and the water that infiltrates within the soil and is stored or runs over the surface when it comes in excess. We consider here a simplified crop irrigation model, inspired from [Pelak, Revelli & Porporato 2017; Kalboussi, Roux, Boumaza, et al. 2019], where we explicitly include the dynamics of soil nitrogen, in addition to soil humidity: [SOURCE: Page_130]

𝑆¤ = 𝑘 1 (−𝜑 (𝑡)𝐾𝑆 (𝑆) − (1 − 𝜑 (𝑡)𝐾𝑅 (𝑆) + 𝑘 2𝑢) − 𝑘 5𝑆   ¤                   𝑁 𝐵 = 𝜑 (𝑡)𝐾𝑆 (𝑆) 𝑓 𝑆   𝑁 𝑁¤ = −𝑘 3𝜑 (𝑡)𝐾𝑆 (𝑆) 𝑓       + 𝑘 4𝐶 𝑁in𝑢 𝑆 [SOURCE: Page_130]

where 𝑆 (𝑡) ∈ [0, 1] denotes the soil humidity level and 𝐵(𝑡), 𝑁 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ+ are biomass and nitrogen contents per unit of soil surface, for 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ . As often met in crop modeling, terms 𝜑 (𝑡)𝐾𝑆 (𝑆), (1 − 𝜑 (𝑡))𝐾𝑅 (𝑆) represent crop transpiration and soil evaporation, respectively. Functions 𝐾𝑆 , 𝐾𝑅 are usually piecewise linear non-decreasing from [0, 1] to [0, 1]. The time function 𝜑 : ℝ+ → ℝ+ is the crop radiation interception efficiency, which is usually assumed to be 𝐶 1 and increasing, while 𝑓 : ℝ → [0, 1] is a piecewise 𝐶 1 non-decreasing function, which regulates the growth depending on the nitrogen concentration. The input variable 𝑢 : ℝ → ℝ is the irrigation flow rate. The parameter 𝑘 1 is linked to the porosity of the soil, while 𝑘 2, 𝑘 3, 𝑘 4 are positive conversion parameters. Here, we consider crops in greenhouse so that there is no rains term. Particularities of this model, compared to former works [Kalboussi, Roux, Boumaza, et al. 2019], are (i) to take into account the drainage term 𝑘 5𝑆. This parameter depends on the soil type and represents the water loss percentage. Soil water drainage is an integral aspect of the water balance that needs to be taken into consideration when decision-makers seek to manage crops. (ii) to consider that the irrigated water contains nitrogen with concentration 𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 . This allows considering fertigation systems with 𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 > 0. [SOURCE: Page_130]

130 [SOURCE: Page_130]

3 a robust adaptive observer for crop fertigation model [SOURCE: Page_131]

Typical instances of the functions 𝐾𝑆 , 𝐾𝑅 , 𝑓 (see Figure 3.1a) are:         𝑆 − 𝑆𝑤                                        𝑆 − 𝑆  ℎ 𝐾𝑆 (𝑆) = min 1, max 0, ∗          ,              𝐾𝑅 (𝑆) = min 1, max 0,        , 𝑆 − 𝑆𝑤                                           1 − 𝑆ℎ        𝐶  𝑓 (𝐶) = max 0, min     ,1 𝜂𝑐 [SOURCE: Page_131]

1                         KS           1                        KR       1                  f [SOURCE: Page_131]

S                                     S   0                      C 0       Sw            1                0   Sh               1                   η       1 S*                                                                   c [SOURCE: Page_131]

(a) The water stress function (blue), the evaporation reduction function (green) as a function of soil moisture 𝑆 and the nitrogen uptake limitation function (red). [SOURCE: Page_131]

Figure 3.1: Water stress, evaporation reduction, and nitrogen uptake limitation functions. [SOURCE: Page_131]

where 𝑆 ∗ is the water stress threshold, 𝑆𝑤 is the humidity threshold above which the plant wilts, 𝑆ℎ is the hydroscopic point, and 𝜂𝑐 is the nitrogen saturation coefficient. Not that we assume that the input variable 𝑢 (·) is known, providing the admissible behavior of all variables in this model. The functional applicability of the model has been shown in real-world contexts, as illustrated in [Kefi et al. 2023], [Haddon, Rapaport, et al. 2023], and [Kalboussi, Roux, Cheviron, et al. 2018]. [SOURCE: Page_131]

3.5.1 states and parameters estimation [SOURCE: Page_131]

The different uncertainties in the parameters of the model (notably for the soil part), the inaccuracies of the agronomic sensors, and the prohibitive cost of the nitrogen measurement sensors are the principal problems encountered in utilizing crop models. In this section, our goal is to build an adaptive observer for reconstructing the uncertain parameters 𝑘 1, 𝑘 2, 𝑘 5 from the water balance and evaluating simultaneously the parame- ter 𝑘 3 and the nitrogen concentration in the whole system. Online measurements are the humidity and the biomass: [SOURCE: Page_131]

𝑦1 (𝑡) = 𝑆 (𝑡) + 𝑣 1 (𝑡), 𝑦2 (𝑡) = 𝐵(𝑡) + 𝑣 2 (𝑡), [SOURCE: Page_131]

3 a robust adaptive observer for crop fertigation model [SOURCE: Page_132]

where 𝑣 1 , 𝑣 2 ∈ L∞ 1 are unknown measurement noises. We shall consider the beginning [SOURCE: Page_132]

of the season (𝑡 = 0) for which the initial humidity level 𝑆 (0) can be assumed to be high, i.e., larger than the threshold 𝑆 ★ and the unknown initial nitrogen concentration can be small, i.e., such that 𝑁 (0)/𝑆 (0) < 𝜂𝑐 . We then look for two estimation problems on a time window for which 𝑆 stays above 𝑆 ★ and 𝑁 /𝑆 below 𝜂𝑐 . In this sub-domain of the state space, the dynamics can be written using the expressions of 𝐾𝑆 , 𝐾𝑅 , and 𝑓 given above, as follows: [SOURCE: Page_132]

(3.6)                𝑆¤ = 𝑘 1 (−𝜑 (𝑡) − (1 − 𝜑 (𝑡))𝐾𝑅 (𝑆) + 𝑘 2𝑢) − 𝑘 5𝑆 𝑁 (3.7)                𝐵¤ = 𝜑 (𝑡) 𝜂𝑐 𝑆 𝑁 (3.8)               𝑁¤ = −𝑘 3𝜑 (𝑡)        + 𝑘 4𝐶 𝑁in𝑢 𝜂𝑐 𝑆 [SOURCE: Page_132]

One can notice that the system has a cascade structure, i.e., the dynamics of the first variable 𝑆 is independent of 𝐵 and 𝑁 . We consider then two estimation problems: A. reconstruct parameters 𝑘 1 , 𝑘 2 , 𝑘 5 related to soil characteristics and the humidity dynamics (3.6) with the single measurement 𝑦1 , assuming that the parameter 𝑆ℎ and functions 𝜑, 𝑢 are known; B. reconstruct the nitrogen variable 𝑁 and parameter 𝑘 3 (related to soil-crop system) with both measurements 𝑦1 , 𝑦2 , assuming that parameters 𝜂𝑐 , 𝑘 4𝐶 𝑁𝑖𝑛 and functions 𝜑, 𝑢 are known. [SOURCE: Page_132]

3.7 conclusion [SOURCE: Page_147]

This work proposed an adaptive observer for a class of nonlinear time-varying systems with disturbances and measurement noises, extending existing results by considering a generic and complex framework. The originality of this contribution is that the regressor depends on the unmeasured states, with time dependency on the system. The analysis of convergence and robustness of this type of adaptive observer used the SIIOS-Lyapunov function method. A crop irrigation model was used to illustrate the observer’s efficiency using numerical simulations. Our motivation to consider this estimation problem is [SOURCE: Page_147]

147 [SOURCE: Page_147]

3 a robust adaptive observer for crop fertigation model [SOURCE: Page_148]

related to the environmental risk of irrigation with the addition of nitrogen, the excess of which can contaminate the soil and pollute the groundwater. In a recent work, we solved the problem of minimizing the total amount of water for irrigation, which guarantees the maximum biomass at the harvest date [Dadjo, Rapaport, Harmand, et al. 2024]. Optimal solutions are expressed as feedback depending on the nitrogen in the soil, which a priori is unavailable. Another difficulty is related to the various uncertainties in the model parameters. Applying our proposed recursive algorithm for the joint estimation of nitrogen and the uncertain parameters brings concrete answers to these challenges, and will the matter of future investigations. [SOURCE: Page_148]

4 practical observability and observer for nonlinear systems subject . . . [SOURCE: Page_150]

Abstract: This chapter investigates the method, we demonstrate how to construct problem of observability and state recon-     an observer such that the estimation error struction for nonlinear systems subject to    converges in a "practical" sense. Several ex- unknown disturbances. We introduce def-       amples are provided to illustrate the con- initions of practical observability in this   cepts and validate the results. context and propose two types of practical observers. Under certain assumptions, by Key words: Practical observability; Prac- tical observer; 𝜀-Practical observability;𝜀- employing the Lyapunov stability analysis Practical observer; SIIOS-stability. [SOURCE: Page_150]

4.1 introduction [SOURCE: Page_150]

The problem of observer design for nonlinear systems is far from having a general solution [Bernard 2017]. As opposed to linear systems, where, for instance, checking the rank of the Kalman observability matrix is necessary and sufficient [Gauthier & Kupka 2004], there is no universal definition of observability for nonlinear systems. This property also depends on the inputs, an essential aspect in nonlinear settings. Most solutions are proposed for the case of uniform input observability (this greatly simplifies the designer’s job since input selection can be avoided). Nevertheless, many existing results are devoted to the simultaneous choice of the input and state estimation, with posterior regulation [Shim & Teel 2003]. In all these cases, it is assumed that if some conditions are satisfied, then a unique state vector can be recovered by a suitable method. Therefore, numerous observers have been synthesized in the literature, providing state estimators with various performances and for different models [Gauthier & Kupka 2004; Shim & Teel 2003]. However, in many cases, these hypotheses of state observability cannot be verified, especially if the system model contains a bounded uncertainty, non-invertible nonlinearities, or variable singular gains. In this work, we consider classes of controlled nonlinear systems under unknown disturbances, which are not observable in common sense and without the hypothesis on the uniformity of observability in the control inputs. We aim to characterize cases where estimating the state vector remains possible with a bounded error in a "practical" sense. In such a setting, the goal is to construct a "practical observer," which converges towards a small neighborhood of the actual state variable. Several design variants of these observers have been already proposed in the literature [Rapaport & Gouzé 2003]. This chapter is structured as follows. We begin with background on classical observ- ability for nonlinear systems in Section 4.2 emphasizing advancements on practical observers. In Section 4.3, we propose the definition of practical observability and three [SOURCE: Page_150]

notation [SOURCE: Page_151]
