• S𝑛 denotes the set of symmetric positive definite matrices of dimension 𝑛 × 𝑛. • 𝐼𝑛 denotes the identity matrix of dimension 𝑛 × 𝑛. • ∥ · ∥ denotes the Euclidean norm for vectors and the induced norm for matrices. • For a Lebesgue measurable function 𝜐 : ℝ+ → ℝ𝑚 , define the norm ∥𝜐 ∥ [𝑡1,𝑡2 ) := ess sup𝑡 ∈[𝑡1,𝑡2 ) ∥𝜐 (𝑡)∥ for [𝑡 1 , 𝑡 2 ) ⊆ ℝ+ . We denote by L𝑚 ∞ the set of functions 𝜐 with ∥𝜐 ∥ ∞ :=∥𝜐 ∥ [0,∞) < +∞. n                       o • For any 𝜉 ∈ ℝ𝑛 , 𝐵𝜀 (𝜉) = 𝑧 ∈ ℝ𝑛 , ∥𝜉 − 𝑧 ∥ ≤ 𝜀 . [SOURCE: Page_151]

• int(𝐸) denotes the interior of the topological space 𝐸. • A continuous function 𝛼 : ℝ+ → ℝ+ belongs to the class K if 𝛼 (0) = 0 and 𝛼 is strictly increasing. The function 𝛼 : ℝ+ → ℝ+ belongs to the class K∞ if 𝛼 ∈ K and it is increasing to infinity. A continuous function 𝛽 : ℝ+ × ℝ+ → ℝ+ belongs to the class KL if 𝛽 (·, 𝑡) ∈ K∞ for each fixed 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ and 𝛽 (𝑠, ·) decreases to zero as 𝑡 → +∞ for each fixed 𝑠 ∈ ℝ∗+ . [SOURCE: Page_151]

4 practical observability and observer for nonlinear systems subject . . . [SOURCE: Page_152]

4.2 preliminaries [SOURCE: Page_152]

This section recalls notions of observability for a system (4.1)                         𝑥¤ (𝑡) = 𝑓 (𝑥 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑣 (𝑡)), 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+, 𝑦 (𝑡) = ℎ(𝑥 (𝑡)), where 𝑥 (𝑡) ∈ X ⊂ ℝ𝑛 is the state vector, 𝑢 (𝑡) ∈ U ⊂ ℝ𝑚 is the control (that can be assigned by a designer), 𝑣 (𝑡) ∈ V ⊂ ℝ𝑞 is an external perturbation and 𝑦 (𝑡) ∈ Y ⊂ ℝ𝑝 is the output vector available for measurements. The map 𝑓 : X × U × V → ℝ𝑛 is Lipschitz continuous with respect to 𝑥 and continuous with respect to other arguments. The map ℎ : X → Y is continuously differentiable. We assume that the set X is forwardly invariant by (4.1) whatever are Lebesgue measurable functions 𝑢, 𝑣 taking values in U, V, respectively. Therefore, for given 𝑥 0 ∈ X, and functions 𝑢 and 𝑣, the corresponding solution 𝑥 (𝑡, 𝑢, 𝑣) to (4.1) is uniquely defined for all 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ . We shall denote by 𝑦𝑥 0,𝑢,𝑣 the output function of (4.1) for the initial condition 𝑥 (0) = 𝑥 0 , the control 𝑢 and the disturbance 𝑣. [SOURCE: Page_152]

4.2.1 observability [SOURCE: Page_152]

In absence of disturbance (say 𝑣 is identically null), the system (4.1) is classically said to be observable for a given input function 𝑢 if two different initial conditions 𝑥 0𝑎 ≠ 𝑥 0𝑏 are distinguishable in finite time, meaning there exists 𝜏 > 0 such that 𝑦𝑥 0𝑎 ,𝑢,0 (𝜏) ≠ 𝑦𝑥 𝑏 ,𝑢,0 (𝜏), 0 [SOURCE: Page_152]

or equivalently, if 𝑦𝑥 0𝑎 ,𝑢,0 (𝑡) = 𝑦𝑥 𝑏 ,𝑢,0 (𝑡) for any 𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ implies 𝑥 0𝑎 = 𝑥 0𝑏 (see, e.g., [Sontag 0 1998]). The system is observable in time 𝛼 (for a given 𝑢) if the initial condition 𝑥 0 can be uniquely reconstructed from the measurements of the output 𝑦 over a finite time interval [0, 𝛼] (see [Sontag 1998]), which implies that there exists a map Ω such that the state 𝑥 (𝑡) is recovered as 𝑥 (𝑡) = Ω 𝑦𝑥 0,𝑢,0 ([𝑡 − 𝛼, 𝑡]), 𝑢 ([𝑡 − 𝛼, 𝑡]) , 𝑡 ≥ 𝛼 .  (4.2) [SOURCE: Page_152]

If the above property is satisfied for any admissible input 𝑢, then the system is called uniformly observable (in time 𝛼). In the presence of unknown (bounded) disturbances 𝑣 . 0, the problem becomes much more challenging, apart from particular cases of exact decoupling of the "unknown inputs" [Hou & Muller 1992; Takahashi & Peres 1999; Rapaport & Gouzé 2003] (property of observability for unknown inputs). In such cases, the influence of disturbances can be canceled in new coordinates, and an exact state estimation remains possible. [SOURCE: Page_152]

152 [SOURCE: Page_152]

4 practical observability and observer for nonlinear systems subject . . . [SOURCE: Page_153]

4.2.2 practical observers in the literature [SOURCE: Page_153]

When the system is not observable for unknown input, and the uncertainty 𝑣 is bounded, concepts of practical observers (see, e.g., [Rapaport & Gouzé 2003]) have been introduced to estimate the state up to some bounded error, with systems of the general form [SOURCE: Page_153]

𝑧¤ (𝑡) = 𝑔(𝑧 (𝑡), 𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡)),   𝑥b(𝑡) = ℓ (𝑧 (𝑡), 𝑦 (𝑡)), [SOURCE: Page_153]

where 𝑧 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑟 is the internal state of the observer and 𝑥b(𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 is the estimate of 𝑥 (𝑡). If for any 𝜀 > 0 there exists a system of this form such that on has [SOURCE: Page_153]

∀𝑥 (0), 𝑧 (0), ∃𝑇 > 0 such that ∥b 𝑥 (𝑡) − 𝑥 (𝑡)∥ ≤ 𝜀, ∀𝑡 ≥ 𝑇 [SOURCE: Page_153]

then, this family of estimators parameterized by 𝜀 is a weak practical observer in the sense proposed in [Rapaport & Gouzé 2003] (the authors considered the case without the control input 𝑢). A weak practical observer is a strong practical observer if the family of systems verifies [SOURCE: Page_153]

∀𝜀 > 0, ∃𝜆𝜀 > 0, ∃𝑘𝜀 > 0, ∀𝑥 (0), 𝑧 (0), ∀𝑡 ≥ 0, 𝑥 (𝑡) − 𝑥 (𝑡)∥ ≤ 𝜀 + 𝑒 −𝜆𝜀 𝑡 (𝑘𝜀 ∥b ∥b                                 𝑥 (0) − 𝑥 (0)∥ − 𝜀) [SOURCE: Page_153]

with 𝜆𝜀 → +∞ as 𝜀 → 0. This concept of a practical observer has been developed and found useful in the context of "interval observers", when the uncertainty 𝑣 belongs to a known product of bounded intervals, [Gouzé, Rapaport & Hadj-Sadok 2000; Awais et al. 2021], and otherwise later exploited in [Benabdallah, Kharrat & Vivalda 2008] (without control inputs). To conclude this section, note that there is no universal definition for "practical ob- servers." Instead, this definition may exist for certain classes of dynamical systems. [SOURCE: Page_153]

4.3 practical observability [SOURCE: Page_153]

To the best of our knowledge, there is no result in the literature dealing with practical observability of systems in the general form of (4.1), that is, to formally quantify the possibility of state estimation with a bounded error only. To this end, in the present work, we propose to relax property (4.2) as follows: [SOURCE: Page_153]

Définition 4.1. [SOURCE: Page_153]

153 [SOURCE: Page_153]

4 practical observability and observer for nonlinear systems subject . . . [SOURCE: Page_154]

(i) Let 𝜀 > 0. The system (4.1) is 𝜀-PO for a given control 𝑢 if there exist 𝛼 > 0 and a set-valued map Ω𝜀 : Y[𝑡−𝛼,𝑡] × U[𝑡−𝛼,𝑡] ⇒ X 1 such that, for any 𝑥 0 ∈ X and disturbance 𝑣, one has [SOURCE: Page_154]

Ω𝜀 𝑦𝑥 0,𝑢,𝑣 ([𝑡 − 𝛼, 𝑡]), 𝑢 ([𝑡 − 𝛼, 𝑡]) ⊂ 𝐵𝜀 (𝑥 (𝑡)), 𝑡 ≥ 𝛼 .  (4.3) [SOURCE: Page_154]

(ii) The system (4.1) is SPO if for any 𝜀 > 0, there exists a control 𝑢𝜀 such that (4.1) is 𝜀-PO. [SOURCE: Page_154]

It is well known that nonlinear systems can have "bad" inputs for which they are not observable [Gauthier & Kupka 2004]. These inputs should be avoided (see, for instance, recent works [Brivadis, Gauthier, Sacchelli & Serres 2021; Brivadis, Gauthier & Sacchelli 2023]). This is the reason why our definitions of practical observability depend on the control 𝑢. To illustrate cases where this notion of practical observability is fulfilled, let us consider a nonlinear system (4.1) written as [SOURCE: Page_154]

𝑥¤1 (𝑡) = 𝑓1 (𝑥 1 (𝑡), 𝑥 2 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡)), 𝑣 (𝑡)) (4.4)                          𝑥¤2 (𝑡) = 𝑓2 (𝑥 1 (𝑡), 𝑥 2 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑣 (𝑡)) 𝑦 (𝑡) = 𝑥 1 (𝑡), 𝑡 ≥ 0, [SOURCE: Page_154]

where 𝑥 1 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛1 , 𝑥 2 (𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛2 , with 𝑛 = 𝑛 1 + 𝑛 2 , and V is a compact set. For the system (4.4), since 𝑥 1 is measured directly, it is necessary to recover the variable 𝑥 2 only. To this end, we may resolve the first equation with respect to 𝑥 2 , considering 𝑥 1 and 𝑥¤1 as known variables (the output and its derivative). Suppose such an inversion admits a unique solution, and it depends on known variables only (i.e., 𝑦,          ¤ 𝑦 and 𝑢). In that case, the system is observable in the infinitesimal sense (i.e. the algebraic observability criterion [Diop & Fliess 1991] is fulfilled). If this inverse map depends on 𝑣, then we can consider the practical observability in the sense of (4.3), provided that the structure of V allows to hide the influence of 𝑣 in 𝜀. However, everything depends on the properties of the dynamics of 𝑥 2 since we may recover this state component with an accuracy depending on the characteristics of the right-hand side of (4.4) and the presence of uncertainty. In some cases, the inversion can be ill-defined. For instance, the variable 𝑥 2 may appear in 𝑓1 within a function 𝜑 : ℝ𝑛2 → ℝ𝑤 for some integer 𝑤 ≥ 1, which has no unique inverse. Popular examples including saturation or dead-zone functions have a set-valued inverse in the constancy domain. In other cases, the variable 𝑥 2 can be multiplied by a singular matrix gain of other known variables, whose inversion is 1 Here Y [𝑡 −𝛼,𝑡 ] , resp. U[𝑡 −𝛼,𝑡 ] is the set of continuous, resp. measurable functions from [𝑡 − 𝛼, 𝑡] to Y, resp. U. [SOURCE: Page_154]

possible only on a subspace, and the remaining variables stay implicitly defined, hence implying practical observability of the state of (4.4). [SOURCE: Page_155]

Let us consider four examples that illustrate different situations depicted in the discus- sion above. [SOURCE: Page_155]

4 practical observability and observer for nonlinear systems subject . . . [SOURCE: Page_156]

with X = (0, +∞) × (0, 1) × (0, 𝜌), where 𝜌 ∈ (0, 1]. Note that in this example there is no control or uncertainty, which amounts to consider U = V = {0}. It is straightforward to show that X is positively invariant. From the derivation of the 𝑦¤ output, one gets 𝑥 2 − 𝑥 3 = − 𝑦 and then 𝑑𝑡𝑑 (𝑥 2 − 𝑥 3 ) = −(𝑥 2 − 𝑥 3 ). The system is not observable because it is not possible to distinguish between 𝑥 2 and 𝑥 3 having the same difference, but 𝑥 2 − 𝑥 3 converges exponentially to 0. Therefore, for any 𝜀 > 𝜌, there exists 𝛼 such that for any initial condition in X one has 𝑥𝑖 (𝑡) ∈ (0, 𝜀) for 𝑖 = 2, 3 and 𝑡 ≥ 𝛼. Hence, it is 𝜀-PO (but not SPO). [SOURCE: Page_156]

Example 4.4. (non-invertible output dynamics on a sub-domain) [SOURCE: Page_156]

𝑥¤1 = min{1, 𝑥 2 }, 𝑥¤2 = 𝑥 2 (2 − 𝑥 2 + 𝑢 + 𝑣), 𝑦 = 𝑥 1, [SOURCE: Page_156]

with X = ℝ+ × [0, 3 + 𝛿], U = [−1, 1], and V = [−𝛿, 𝛿]. One can easily check that X is forwardly invariant. Note that 𝑥 2 (𝑡) < 1 implies 𝑦¤ (𝑡) < 1 and then one has 𝑥 2 (𝑡) = 𝑦¤ (𝑡). Otherwise, whatever is the control 𝑢, there exists a perturbation 𝑣 such that the solution verifies 𝑥 2 (𝑡) ≥ 1 for all 𝑡 ∈ ℝ∗+ : take, for instance, 𝑣 ≡ 𝛿 and one has 𝑥¤2 ≥ 𝑥 2 (1 − 𝑥 2 + 𝛿) and 𝑥 2 = 1 is thus repulsive. It is not possible to distinguish 𝑥 2 (𝑡) in the interval [1, 1 + 𝛿]. The system is thus not SPO. With the constant control 𝑢 = −1, the solution 𝑥 2 converges asymptotically to the interval [1 − 𝛿, 1 + 𝛿]. The system is then 𝜀-PO 𝜀-PO for any 𝜀 > 𝛿 with this control: either 𝑥 2 (𝑡) takes a value below 1 at a time 𝑡 ∈ ℝ∗+ , and 𝑥 2 is exactly reconstructed, either 𝑥 2 stays equal or above 1 and reaches the interval [1, 1 + 𝜀] in finite time, for any 𝑣. One has ( {𝑦 (𝑡)} × [1, 1 + 𝜀], 𝑦¤ (𝑡) = 1 Ω𝜀 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑦¤ (𝑡)) = {(𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑦¤ (𝑡))} ,   𝑦¤ (𝑡) < 1 [SOURCE: Page_156]

Note that when 𝜀 is arbitrary closed to 𝛿, 𝛼 tends to +∞: for an initial condition 𝑥 2 (0) ∈ (1 + 𝛿, 3 + 𝛿) and 𝑣 ≡ 𝛿, the solution 𝑥 2 (𝑡) is outside the interval [1, 1 + 𝛿] for any 𝑡. [SOURCE: Page_156]

Example 4.5. (non-uniformity in the control) [SOURCE: Page_156]

Therefore, for any 𝜀 ∈ (0, 𝛿) one can take the (constant) control 𝑢𝜀 = 𝛿𝜀 − 1 > 0 so that 𝑥 2 (𝑡) ∈ 𝐵𝜀 (−𝑦¤ (𝑡)/𝑦 (𝑡)) for any 𝑡 ∈ ℝ∗+ . The system is thus SPO, but differently to Example 4.2, the control 𝑢𝜀 depends on the parameter 𝜀. [SOURCE: Page_157]

4.4 practical observers [SOURCE: Page_157]

The definition of practical observability implies a new formulation of the corresponding practical observer design problem. [SOURCE: Page_157]

Definition 4.6. Let us consider a family of dynamical systems parameterized by 𝜀 > 0: [SOURCE: Page_157]

𝑧¤ (𝑡) = 𝑔𝜀 (𝑧 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡), 𝑦 (𝑡)) (4.13) 𝑥b(𝑡) = ℓ𝜀 (𝑧 (𝑡), 𝑦 (𝑡)), 𝑡 ≥ 0, [SOURCE: Page_157]

where 𝑧 (𝑡) ∈ Z ⊂ ℝ𝑟 (for some integer 𝑟 ) is the internal state of the observer, and 𝑥b(𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 is the state estimate. [SOURCE: Page_157]

(i) Let 𝜀 > 0. The system (4.13) is an 𝜀-PObs for the system (4.1) with a control 𝑢𝜀 (·), if there exist functions 𝛽𝜀 ∈ KL, 𝛾𝜀 ∈ K and numbers 𝜆𝜀 , 𝜇𝜀 > 0, such that for any (𝑥 0, 𝑧 0 ) ∈ X × Z and disturbance 𝑣 (·), one has [SOURCE: Page_157]

𝑥 (𝑡) − 𝑥 (𝑡)∥ ≤ 𝜀 + 𝛽𝜀 (∥b ∥b                         𝑥 (0) − 𝑥 0 ∥, 𝜆𝜀 𝑡)  + 𝛾𝜀 𝜇𝜀 ∥𝑣 ∥ [0,𝑡) , 𝑡 > 0. [SOURCE: Page_157]

4 practical observability and observer for nonlinear systems subject . . . [SOURCE: Page_158]

(ii) The system (4.13) is a SPObs if it is an 𝜀-PObs for any 𝜀 > 0 with 𝜆𝜀 → ∞, 𝜇𝜀 → 0 when 𝜀 → 0. [SOURCE: Page_158]

Let us underline that in the present definition of practical observers, the control 𝑢 may depend on 𝜀 (denoted by 𝑢𝜀 ). At the same time, only asymptotic convergence is required, unlike other definitions of practical observers that require an exponential convergence property, uniformly in 𝑢 [Rapaport & Gouzé 2003; Benabdallah, Kharrat & Vivalda 2008]. Moreover, the influence of the uncertainty 𝑣 is quantified separately. In this sense, this definition is weaker than the one in the literature. In the framework of the theory of input-to-output stable systems, the above requirement may be viewed as a practical state-independent input-to-output stability [Sontag & Wang 1999], where additional attention is given to the steady-state estimation error through the 𝜀-dependent gains 𝜆𝜀 and 𝜇𝜀 . [SOURCE: Page_158]

4.4.1 observer design [SOURCE: Page_158]

Consider a nonlinear system of the form: [SOURCE: Page_158]

𝑥¤ (𝑡) = 𝐴𝑥 (𝑡) + 𝜑 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡)) + 𝐷 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡))𝜙 (𝐻𝑥 (𝑡)) + 𝑣 (𝑡), (4.14)       𝑦 (𝑡) = 𝐶𝑥 (𝑡), ∀𝑡 ∈ ℝ+, [SOURCE: Page_158]

where 𝑥 (𝑡) ∈ X ⊂ ℝ𝑛 , 𝑢 (𝑡) ∈ U ⊂ ℝ𝑚 , 𝑦 (𝑡) ∈ Y ⊂ ℝ𝑝 and 𝑣 (𝑡) ∈ V ⊂ ℝ𝑙 . The matrices 𝐴 ∈ ℝ𝑛×𝑛 , 𝐶 ∈ ℝ𝑝×𝑛 and 𝐻 ∈ ℝ𝑤×𝑛 are given, 𝜑 : ℝ𝑝 × ℝ𝑚 → ℝ𝑛 , 𝜙 : ℝ𝑤 → ℝ𝑞 , 𝐷 : ℝ𝑝 × ℝ𝑚 → ℝ𝑛×𝑞 are locally Lipschitz continuous functions (with vector and matrix values). Below, we will assume the detectability of the pair (𝐴, 𝐶), which does not imply the observability of (4.14). Moreover, we suppose that the system is not observable but that there exists another map Φ : ℝ𝑤 → ℝ𝑞 , so that the practical observability is satisfied when 𝜙 is replaced by Φ, under the following assumptions: [SOURCE: Page_158]

Assumption 4.7. Given an input function 𝑢 : ℝ+ → U, there exists Υ ∈ S𝑞 such that [SOURCE: Page_158]

𝐷 ⊤ (𝑦, 𝑢 (𝑡))𝐷 (𝑦, 𝑢 (𝑡)) ≤ Υ, ∀𝑦 ∈ Y, ∀𝑡 ∈ ℝ+ . [SOURCE: Page_158]

4 practical observability and observer for nonlinear systems subject . . . [SOURCE: Page_159]

Assumption 4.8. There exists 𝜀 1 > 0 such that ∥𝜙 (𝐻𝑥) − Φ(𝐻𝑥)∥ ≤ 𝜀 1, ∀𝑥 ∈ X, where the function Φ verifies the quadratic constraint for any 𝑠 1, 𝑠 2 ∈ ℝ𝑤 :                    ⊤                          𝑠1 − 𝑠2            𝐸   𝑅      𝑠1 − 𝑠2 ≥ −𝜀 2 Φ(𝑠 1 ) − Φ(𝑠 2 )     𝑅 ⊤ −2Υ Φ(𝑠 1 ) − Φ(𝑠 2 ) for some matrices 𝐸 ∈ S𝑤 , 𝑅 ∈ ℝ𝑤×𝑞 and 𝜀 2 ≥ 0. [SOURCE: Page_159]

Here, Assumption 4.7 introduces a particular input under which the estimation will be possible, while Assumption 4.8 clarifies the origins of practical observability: the map Φ that is introduced instead of 𝜙 is at a certain distance 𝜀 1 from 𝜙, and there is an 𝜀 2 relaxation in the quadratic constraint that will be used to design an observer with Φ instead of 𝜙. The practical observer is introduced in the form for 𝑡 ≥ 0: (4.15)           ¤ (𝑡) = 𝐴b 𝑥b        𝑥 (𝑡) + 𝜑 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡)) + 𝐿(𝑦 (𝑡) − 𝐶b𝑥 (𝑡)) +𝐷 (𝑦 (𝑡), 𝑢 (𝑡))Φ (𝐻b 𝑥 (𝑡) + 𝑀 (𝑦 (𝑡) − 𝐶b𝑥 (𝑡))) , where 𝑥b(𝑡) ∈ ℝ𝑛 is the state estimate, the matrices 𝑀 ∈ ℝ𝑤×𝑝 and 𝐿 ∈ ℝ𝑛×𝑝 being the observer gains to be designed. [SOURCE: Page_159]

Remark 4.9. Note that 𝜙 may also be uncertain. We need to know only that the map Φ that should be close to 𝜙 (in a similar way as in [Benabdallah, Kharrat & Vivalda 2008]). [SOURCE: Page_159]

The main result of this section is as follows. We omit its proof for brevity. [SOURCE: Page_159]

This theorem illustrates a situation where the proposed notions of PO and PObs can be relevant for a system that is practically observable under the substitution of a part in its dynamics by another component with a bounded error with respect to the original term. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Remark 4.12. Among 𝑀, 𝑃, 𝑈 and 𝛼, 𝛾 such that the LMI of Theorem 4.10 is fulfilled, one can look for the optimization problem that consists in minimizing √︃ 𝜀 = (2𝛾𝜆max (Υ)𝜀 12 + 𝜀 2 )/(𝛼𝜆min (𝑃)). [SOURCE: Page_161]

This presents some similitude with 𝐻 ∞ robust control for which one searches for the best attenuation factor 𝛾. However, here, one has to optimize several parameters. Development of this optimization problem might be a purpose of future work. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Example 1. Consider the system (4.14) with 𝑛 = 3 and  −1 1    0          𝑢                                     𝐴 = −2 −3 0  , 𝜑 (𝑦, 𝑢) = 0 , 𝐶 = 1 0 0 ,                                                0 0 −2              𝑢                                 ⊤             𝐷 = 0 −1 0 , 𝐻 = 0 1 0 , 𝜙 (𝐻𝑥) = tanh (𝑥 2 ), and                                                               ⊤ 𝑣 (𝑡) = 𝑣 1 (𝑡) 𝑣 2 (𝑡) 𝑣 3 (𝑡) . Only the first state variable is directly measurable, while the second is reconstructable in a linear way, but the third one is not, since its computation from the second equation depends on the uncertainty 𝑣. Nevertheless, one can practically recover the third state in the sense of 4.3. 4.6 conclusion [SOURCE: Page_164]

In this work, we have introduced the notions of 𝜀-Po and SPo for nonlinear systems subject to bounded disturbances. The goal was to relax the uniformly observable prop- erty and to recover the state vector up to a ball of radius 𝜀. These notions have been illustrated in four different examples. The construction of an 𝜀-PObs has also been proposed for a class of nonlinear systems. The analysis of the estimation error was conducted for this class of systems using the Lyapunov function method. The design of 𝜀-PObs has been illustrated in the examples. [SOURCE: Page_164]

## GENERAL CONCLUSION AND PERSPECTIVES

5.1 general conclusion [SOURCE: Page_165]

The research presented in the first part of this manuscript represents a key step toward the development of a decision-making support tool for fertigation management under water scarcity conditions, particularly through the integration of reuse strategies. We proposed optimal fertigation strategies based on a crop model, viability analysis, and optimal control techniques. The crop model describes water and nitrogen stress and is suitable for use as a decision support tool for irrigation management. The viability analysis conducted using the model enables a precise characterization of the ranges of nitrogen concentration in irrigation water and the maximum irrigation rate for which the viability property is satisfied. In other words, it identifies the conditions under which both water stress and nitrogen stress are avoided, thereby ensuring maximal biomass production. We have characterized control strategies aimed at achieving maximum veg- etable production (i.e., maintaining viability) while minimizing total water consumption. We identified a variety of markedly different control strategies, which are illustrated in Chapitre 2, depending on threshold values of the initial nitrogen concentrations. For high initial nitrogen, it is not surprising that the nitrogen constraint remains inactive. In contrast, for low initial nitrogen, the lack of irrigation leads to nitrogen stress before hydric stress, which is non-intuitive. This strategy relies on the existence of a date during the season that leads the system to have the nitrogen constraint active exactly at the final time. [SOURCE: Page_165]

165 [SOURCE: Page_165]

5 general conclusion and perspectives [SOURCE: Page_166]

Other non-intuitive strategies involve keeping the system close to the nitrogen con- straint while avoiding the feasibility boundaries, when nitrogen stress may prevent reaching the humidity threshold. Other optimal behaviors include arcs located inside the viability domain. These cor- respond to optimal singular strategies, constructed as convex combinations of two extreme controls that should reach the corner point of the viability domain at the final time. An infinite number of optimal controls can be constructed around this solution by introducing small admissible perturbations. These singular controls demonstrate that even without forcing the constraints, optimal solutions can emerge through internal dynamic balance. These strategies show that the maximum production constraint does not require continuous or maximal irrigation, but rather a fine regulation of the system, allowing the agronomic and economic objectives to be optimally achieved. These strategies aim to adjust water and nitrogen inputs in order to maximize biomass production, while accounting for the initial amount of nitrogen available in the soil. In Chapitre 3 of this thesis, the proposed observer extends existing results by addressing the challenging case where the regressor depends explicitly on the unmeasured state variables. Using a Lyapunov-based approach, we established sufficient conditions for the convergence of the estimation error in the presence of bounded disturbances. The robust adaptive observer was developed to jointly estimate the initial amount of nitrogen and uncertain parameters of the model. The model is formulated as a class of nonlinear non-autonomous systems, where the available output measurements are soil moisture and vegetable biomass production. The regressor depends on an unmeasured state. The robustness and mean convergence of the proposed adaptive observer in the presence of noise have been established. By tailoring existing results to the crop fertigation model, the observer achieves improved robustness and faster convergence. In the Chapitre 4 of this thesis, we introduced a framework for the practical observability of nonlinear systems subject to bounded disturbances. We proposed new definitions of practical observability and practical observers, which extend classical observability concepts by allowing for state estimation within a bounded error. These definitions are well-suited for systems with structural uncertainties or unknown inputs, where exact observability is not achievable. We designed a class of practical observers and established their convergence properties using Lyapunov-based analysis. We also provided sufficient conditions in the form of linear matrix inequalities that ensure the robustness and performance of the proposed observer. [SOURCE: Page_166]

166 [SOURCE: Page_166]

5 general conclusion and perspectives [SOURCE: Page_167]

These results confirm that practical observers can provide reliable state estimates even under severe uncertainties, and open the way for their application in a wide range of control systems. [SOURCE: Page_167]

5.2 perspectives [SOURCE: Page_167]

The PhD period is not limited to writing the dissertation and preparing for the defense. It also opens up promising research directions that I intend to further explore and develop. Several avenues have already been identified: [SOURCE: Page_167]

towards more realistic fertigation modeling [SOURCE: Page_167]

a) The current fertigation model does not incorporate climate variability, which is inherently uncertain. To enhance realism, it is crucial to integrate probabilistic climate scenarios, enabling real-time decision-making; b) Water and nitrogen losses due to drainage should be explicitly accounted for, to better reflect the environmental impact of fertigation practices; c) The model can be extended to an infinite-dimensional setting, allowing for a finer spatial and temporal representation of soil–plant interactions; [SOURCE: Page_167]

optimal control under constraints [SOURCE: Page_167]

As developed in Chapitre 2, one major challenge lies in solving an optimal control problem under state constraints, with nonlinear and non-smooth dynamics. We initially focused on establishing a sufficient condition based on viability theory ensuring that state constraints are satisfied to maximize vegetable biomass production at harvest time. [SOURCE: Page_167]

a) A natural next step is to characterize the viability kernel (see Definition 2.2) and deduce the associated optimal solutions; b) Applying stochastic optimization techniques in this context could help determine optimal strategies under uncertain environmental conditions. [SOURCE: Page_167]

167 [SOURCE: Page_167]

5 general conclusion and perspectives [SOURCE: Page_168]

hybrid modeling for wastewater treatment systems [SOURCE: Page_168]

Another promising research avenue concerns wastewater treatment systems, which currently lack a dynamic output model. Our objective is to design a simplified model based on a switched system framework, representing two operational modes: either nitrogen conservation for agronomic reuse, or complete removal of nitrogen and carbon before environmental discharge. The controllability of such a hybrid system will be investigated. A further step will involve coupling this model with a crop growth model, in order to assess integrated scenarios for combined treatment-agriculture systems. [SOURCE: Page_168]

towards improved crop modeling and estimation [SOURCE: Page_168]

The crop model used so far presents several limitations. In particular, it does not account for excess nitrogen that may leach into soil and groundwater. To improve the accuracy and environmental relevance of the model, future work could focus on minimizing nitrogen losses resulting from over-irrigation. Due to the lack of uniform observability of the fertigation model outside the set E, extending the practical observer to the non-autonomous case represents a promising direction for estimating soil-available nitrogen under water or nitrogen stress. Another perspective is to optimize the upper bound of the estimation error associated with the 𝜀-PObs observer. Future work will aim at optimizing the observer design by minimizing the estimation error bound, and at extending the estimation framework to broader classes of nonlinear systems. [SOURCE: Page_168]

control strategies based on optimal references [SOURCE: Page_168]

Finally, a major direction for future research would be to exploit the various optimal nitrogen trajectories as reference signals in the design of an adaptive tracking strategy. This would enable the fertigation system to dynamically adjust its behavior according to the real-time needs of the crop. [SOURCE: Page_168]

168 [SOURCE: Page_168]
